Graduality from Embedding-Projection Pairs Note: In this paper, we use blue to typeset our gradual cast calculus λ G \lambda_{G} and red to typeset our typed language with errors λ T , ℧ \lambda_{T,\mho} . The paper will be much easier to read if viewed/printed in color. CCS: Software and its engineering General programming languages CCS: Social and professional topics History of programming languages
Max S. New, Amal Ahmed

Abstract
. Gradually typed languages allow statically typed and dynamically typed code to interact while maintaining benefits of both styles. The key to reasoning about these mixed programs is Siek-Vitousek-Cimini-Boyland’s (dynamic) gradual guarantee, which says that giving components of a program more precise types only adds runtime type checking, and does not otherwise change behavior. In this paper, we give a semantic reformulation of the gradual guarantee called graduality . We change the name to promote the analogy that graduality is to gradual typing what parametricity is to polymorphism. Each gives a local-to-global, syntactic-to-semantic reasoning principle that is formulated in terms of a kind of observational approximation. Utilizing the analogy, we develop a novel logical relation for proving graduality. We show that embedding-projection pairs (ep pairs) are to graduality what relations are to parametricity. We argue that casts between two types where one is “more dynamic” (less precise) than the other necessarily form an ep pair, and we use this to cleanly prove the graduality cases for casts from the ep-pair property. To construct ep pairs, we give an analysis of the type dynamism relation—also known as type precision or naïve subtyping—that interprets the rules for type dynamism as compositional constructions on ep pairs, analogous to the coercion interpretation of subtyping.



Extended Version

1.
Introduction

Gradually typed programming languages are designed to resolve the
conflict between static and dynamically typed programming styles

(
Tobin-Hochstadt
and Felleisen 2006
;
Tobin-Hochstadt
and Felleisen 2008
;
Siek and Taha 2006
)
.
A gradual language allows a smooth transition from dynamic to static
typing through gradual addition of types to dynamically typed
programs, and allows for safe interactions between more statically
typed and more dynamically typed components.
With such an enticing goal there has
been extensive research on gradual typing—e.g.,

(
Siek and Taha 2006
;
Gronski et al. 2006
;
Wadler and
Findler 2009
;
Ina and Igarashi 2011
;
Swamy et al. 2014
;
Allende et al. 2013
)
—with
recent work aimed at extending gradual typing to more advanced
language features, such as parametric
polymorphism
(
Ahmed
et al. 2017
;
Igarashi
et al. 2017a
)
, effect
tracking
(
Bañados Schwerter et al. 2014
)
, typestate
(
Wolff
et al. 2011
)
,
session types
(
Igarashi et al. 2017b
)
, and refinement
types
(
Lehmann and
Tanter 2017
)
.

Formalizing the idea of a “smooth transition”, a key property that
every gradually typed language should satisfy is

Siek
et al. 2015
’s
(dynamic)
1
1

1

The same work also
introduces a
static
gradual guarantee that says that
changing the types in a program to be less dynamic means type
checking becomes stricter. We do not consider this in our paper
because we only consider the semantics of cast calculi, not the
type systems of gradual surface languages. We discuss the
relationship further in §
7

gradual guarantee
, which we refer to as
graduality
(by
analogy with parametricity).
Graduality enables programmers to modify their program from a
dynamically typed to a statically typed style, and vice-versa, with
confidence that the program’s behavior only changes in predictable
ways.
Specifically, it says that changing the types in a
program to be “less dynamic”/”more precise”—i.e., changing from
the dynamic type to some more precise type such as integers or
functions—either produces the same behavior as the original program
or causes a dynamic type error.
Conversely, if a program does not error and some types are made “more
dynamic”/”less precise” then the program has the exact same
behavior. This is an important reasoning principle for programmers as
the alternative would be quite counterintuitive: for instance,
changing certain type annotations might cause a terminating program to
diverge, or a program that prints your calendar to tweet your home
address!
This distinguishes dynamic type checking in gradual typing from
exceptions: raising an exception is a valid program behavior that can
be caught and handled by a caller, whereas a dynamic type error is
always considered to be a bug, and terminates the program.

More formally, the notion of when a type
\sA
is “less dynamic”
than another type
\sB
is specified by a
type dynamism

relation (also known as type precision or naïve subtyping), written

\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
, which is defined for simple languages as the least
congruence relation such that the dynamic type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
is the

most
dynamic type:
\sA\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
.
Then,
term dynamism
(also known as term
precision) is the natural extension of type dynamism to terms, written

\st\sqsubseteq\ss
.
The graduality theorem is then that if
\st\sqsubseteq\ss
, then the
behavior of
\st
must be “less dynamic” than the behavior of

\ss
—that is, either
\st
produces a runtime type error or both
terms have the exact same behavior.
We say
\st
is “less dynamic” in the sense that it has
fewer
behaviors
.

Unfortunately, for the majority of gradually typed languages, the
(dynamic) gradual guarantee is considered quite challenging to prove,
and there is only limited guidance about how to design new languages
so that they satisfy this property. There are two notable exceptions:
Abstracting Gradual Typing (AGT)
(
Garcia
et al. 2016a
)
and the Gradualizer

(
Cimini and Siek 2016
;
Cimini and Siek 2017
)
provide systematic methods and
formal tools, respectively, for deriving a gradually typed language
from a statically typed language, and they both provide the gradual
guarantee by construction. However, while they provide a proof of the
gradual guarantee for languages produced in the respective frameworks,
most gradually typed languages are not produced in this way; for
instance, Typed Racket’s approach to gradual typing

(
Tobin-Hochstadt
and Felleisen 2006
;
Tobin-Hochstadt
and Felleisen 2008
)
is not explained by either system.
Furthermore, both Gradualizer and AGT base their semantics on static
type checking itself, but this is the reverse of the semantic view of
type checking.
In the semantic viewpoint, type checking should be justified by a
sensible semantics, and not the other way around.

Type Dynamism and Embedding-Projection Pairs

While the gradual guarantee as presented in
Siek
et al. 2015
makes type
dynamism a central component, the semantic meaning of type dynamism is
unclear.
This is not just a philosophical question: it is unclear how to extend
type dynamism to new language features.
For instance, polymorphic gradually typed languages have been
developed recently by
Ahmed
et al. 2017
and
Igarashi
et al. 2017a
,
but the two papers have different definitions of type dynamism,
and neither attempts a proof of the (dynamic) gradual guarantee.
The AGT
(
Garcia
et al. 2016a
)
approach gives a systematic definition of
type dynamism in terms of sets of static types, but that definition is
difficult to separate from the rest of their framework, whereas we
would like a definition that can be interpreted in any gradually typed
language.
At present, the best guidance we have comes from the gradual guarantee
itself: the dynamic type should be the greatest element, and the
gradual guarantee should hold.

We propose a semantic definition for type dynamism that naturally
leads to a clean formulation and proof of the gradual guarantee:
An ordering
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
should hold when the casts between the
two types form an
embedding-projection pair
.

What does this mean?
First, in order to support interaction between statically typed and
dynamic typed code while still maintaining the guarantees of the
static types, gradually typed languages include

casts
2
2

2

It is not literally true that every gradual
language uses this presentation of casts from cast calculi, but in
order for a language to be gradually typed, some means of casting
between types must be available, such as embedding dynamic code in
statically typed code, or type annotations. We argue that the
properties of casts we identify here should apply to those
presentations as well.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
that dynamically check if a
value of type
\sA
corresponds to a valid inhabitant of the type

\sB
, and if so, transform its value to have the right type.
Then if
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
, we say that the casts
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
form an
embedding-projection pair
, which means
that they satisfy the following two properties that describe acceptable
behaviors when casting between the two types:
retraction
and

projection
.

First,
\sA
should be a
stricter
type than
\sB
, so anything
satisfying
\sA
should also satisfy
\sB
.
This is captured in the
retraction
property: if we cast a value

\sv:\sA
from
\sA
to
\sB
and then back down to
\sA
, we should
get back an equivalent value because
\sv
should satisfy the type of

\sA
and
\sB
.
Formally,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st\approx\st
where
\approx

means
observational equivalence
of the programs: when placed in
the same spot in a program, they produce the same behavior.

Second, casts should only be doing type
checking
, and not
otherwise changing the behavior of the term.
Since
\sB
is a weaker property than
\sA
, if we cast a value of

\sv:\sB
down to
\sA
, there may be a runtime type error.
However, if
\sv
really does satisfy
\sA
the cast succeeds, and if
we cast back to
\sB
we should get back a value with similar behavior
to
\sv
.
If
\sB
is a first-order type like booleans or numbers, we should get
back exactly the same value.
However, if
\sA,\sB
are higher-order types like functions or
objects, then it is impossible to check if a value of one type
\sB

satisfies
\sA
.
For instance, if
\sB={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and
\sA={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
, then it is not decidable whether or not a value of
\sB

will always return a number on every input.
Instead, following
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
, gradual type casts

wrap
the function with a cast on its outputs and if at any
point it returns something that is not a number, a type error is
raised.
So if
\sv:\sB
is cast to
\sA
and back, we cannot expect to
always get an equivalent value back, but the result should
error
more
—that is, either the cast to
\sA
raises an error, or we get
back a new value
\svpr:\sB
that has the same behavior as
\sv

except it sometimes raises a type error.
We formalize this as
observational error approximation
and write the
ordering
\st\sqsubseteq\stpr
as “
\st
errors more than

\stpr
”.
We then use this to formalize the
projection
property:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\ss\sqsubseteq\ss
.

Notice how the justification for the projection property uses the same
intuition as graduality: that casts should only be doing

checking
and not completely changing a program’s behavior.
This is the key to why embedding-projection pairs help to formulate
and prove graduality: we view graduality as the natural extension of
the projection property from a property of casts to a property of
arbitrary gradually typed programs.

This gives us nice properties of some casts, but what do we know about
casts that are
not
upcasts or downcasts?
In traditional formulations, gradual typing includes casts

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
between types that are
shallowly
compatible
—i.e, that are not guaranteed to fail. For instance, we
can cast a pair where the left side is known to be a number
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
to a type where the right side is known to be a number
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
with casts succeeding on values where both sides are
numbers.
The resulting cast

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
is neither an
upcast nor a downcast.
We argue that the formulation based on these “general” casts is ill
behaved from a meta-theoretic perspective: you are quite limited in
your ability to break casts for larger types into casts for smaller
types.
Most notably, the
composition
of two general casts is very
rarely the same as the direct cast.
For instance, casting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and back
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
always errors, but obviously the direct cast
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}

is the identity.
We show that upcast and downcasts on the other hand satisfy a

decomposition
theorem: if
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sAin{3}
, then the upcast from
\sAone
to
\sAin{3}
factors through

\sAtwo
and similarly for the downcast.

Furthermore, if we disregard
performance
of the casts, and only
care about the observational behavior, we show that any ‘‘general’’
cast is the composition of an upcast followed by a
downcast.
3
3

3

Note that this is not the same as the factorization
of casts known as “threesomes”, see §
7
for a
comparison.

For instance, our cast from before

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
is
observationally equivalent to the composition of first

up
casting to a pair where both sides are dynamically typed

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and then
down
casting:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
.
We show that
all
the casts in a standard gradually typed
language exhibit this factorization, which means that for the purposes
of formulating and proving graduality, we need only discuss upcasts
and downcasts.
For implementation, it is more convenient to have a primitive notion
of coercion/direct cast to eliminate/collapse casts

(
Herman
et al. 2010
;
Siek and Wadler 2010
)
, but we argue that the
correctness of such an implementation should be justified by a
simulation relation with a simpler semantics, meaning the
implementation would inherit a proof of graduality from the simpler
semantics as well.

To prove these equivalence and approximation results, we develop a
novel step-indexed logical relation that is sound for observational
error approximation.
We also develop high-level reasoning principles from the relation so
that our main lemmas do not involve any manual step-manipulation.

Finally, based on our semantic interpretation of type dynamism as
embedding-projection pairs, we provide a refined analysis of the proof
theory of type dynamism as a
syntax for building ep pairs
.
We give a semantics for these proof terms analogous to the coercion
interpretation of subtyping derivations.
Similar to subtyping, we prove a
coherence
theorem which gives,
as a corollary, our decomposition theorem for upcasts and downcasts.

Graduality

In
Siek
et al. 2015
, they prove the (dynamic) gradual guarantee by
an operational simulation argument whose details are quite tied to the
specific cast calculus used.
Using the ep pairs, we provide a more semantic formulation and proof
of graduality.
First, we use our analysis of type dynamism as denoting ep pairs to
define graduality as a kind of observational error approximation

up to upcast/downcast
, building on the axiomatic semantics of
graduality in
New and Licata 2018
.
We then prove the graduality theorem using our logical relation for
error approximation.
Notably, the decomposition theorem for ep pairs leads to a clean,
uniform proof of the cast case of graduality.

Overview of Technical Development and Contributions

In this paper, we show how to prove graduality for a standard gradually typed cast
calculus by translating it into a simple typed language with recursive
types and errors. Specifically, our development proceeds as follows:

(1)

We present a standard gradually typed cast calculus
(
\lambda_{G}
) and its operational semantics, using “general”
casts (§
2
).

(2)

We present a simple typed language with recursive types and a
type error (
\lambda_{T,\mho}
), into which we translate the cast
calculus. Casts in
\lambda_{G}
are translated to contracts
implemented in the typed language (§
3
).

(3)

We develop a novel step-indexed logical relation that is
sound for our notion of observational error approximation
(§
4
). We prove transitivity of the logical
relation and other high-level reasoning principles so that our main
lemmas for ep-pairs and graduality do not involve any manual
step-manipulation.

(4)

We present a novel analysis of type dynamism as a coherent syntax
for ep pairs and show that all of the casts of the gradual language
can be factorized as an upcast followed by a downcast (§
5
).

(5)

We give a semantic formulation of graduality and then prove it using
our error-approximation logical relation and ep pairs
(§
6
).

2.
Gradual Cast Calculus

Our starting point is a fairly typical gradual cast calculus, called

\lambda_{G}
, in the style of
Wadler and
Findler 2009
and

Siek
et al. 2015
.
A cast calculus is usually the target of an elaboration pass from a
gradually typed surface language.
The gradually typed surface language makes mixing static and dynamic
code seamless, for instance a typed function on numbers
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
can be applied to a dynamically typed value

x:\mathord{?}
and the result is well typed
f(x):\mathbb{N}
.
Since
x
is not known to be a number, at runtime a dynamic check is
performed: if
x
is a number,
f
is run with its value and otherwise
a dynamic type error is raised.
In the surface language, this checking behavior takes place at every
elimination form: pattern matching, referencing a field, etc.
The cast calculus makes the dynamic type checking separate from the
elimination forms using explicit cast forms.
If
\st:\sA
in the cast calculus, then we can cast it to another
type
\sB
using the cast form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st
.
This means we can use the ordinary typed reduction rules for
elimination forms, and all the details of checking are isolated to the
cast reductions.
We choose to use a cast calculus, rather than a gradual surface
language, since we are chiefly concerned with the semantics of the
language, rather than gradual type checking.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{Types}&\sty,\styalt&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\sunitty}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\spairty{\sty}{\styalt}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\ssumty{\sty}{\styalt}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\sfunty{\sty}{\styalt}}}\\
\mbox{Tags}&\stagty&\mathrel{\bf::=}&\sunitty\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\\
\mbox{Terms}&\sterm,\stermalt&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\svar}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stunit}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stpair{\stermone}{\stermtwo}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stmatchpair{\sx}{\sy}{\st}{\ss}}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\stinj{\sterm}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stinjpr{\sterm}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\stcase{\sterm}{\svarone}{\stermone}{\svartwo}{\stermtwo}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stfun{\svar}{\sA}{\sterm}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stapp{\st}{\ss}}\\
\mbox{Values}&\sval&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\sval}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stunit}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stpair{\svalone}{\svaltwo}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stinj{\sval}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stinjpr{\sval}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\stfun{\svar}{\sty}{\sterm}}\\
\mbox{Evaluation Contexts}&\sectxt&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{[{}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\cdot}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{]{}}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\sectxt}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stpair{\sectxt}{\ss}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stpair{\sv}{\sectxt}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stmatchpair{\sx}{\sy}{\sectxt}{\ss}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&\stinj{\sectxt}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stinjpr{\sectxt}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\stcase{\sectxt}{\sxone}{\stone}{\sxtwo}{\sttwo}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\sectxt\,\ss\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\sv\,\sectxt\\
\mbox{Environments}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\cdot}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}},{\svar}:{\sty}\\
\mbox{Substitutions}&\sgamma&\mathrel{\bf::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\sgamma,\sv/\sx\end{array}

Figure 1.
\lambda_{G}
Syntax

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sA}

\frac{~}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}}:{\sA}}\frac{~}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}},\sx:\sA,\senvpr}\vdash{}{\sx}:{\sA}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sA}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\st}}:{\sB}}\frac{~}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stunit}:{\sunitty}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stermone}:{\styone}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stermtwo}:{\stytwo}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stpair{\stermone}{\stermtwo}}:{\spairty{\styone}{\stytwo}}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sAtwo}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}},\sx:\sAone,\sy:\sAtwo}\vdash{}{\ss}:{\sB}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stmatchpair{\sx}{\sy}{\st}{\ss}}:{\sB}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sA}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stinj{\st}}:{\ssumty{\sA}{\sApr}}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sApr}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stinjpr{\st}}:{\ssumty{\sA}{\sApr}}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sApr}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}},{\sx}:{\sA}}\vdash{}{\ss}:{\sB}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}},{\sxpr}:{\sApr}}\vdash{}{\sspr}:{\sB}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\stcase{\st}{\sx}{\ss}{\sxpr}{\sspr}}:{\sB}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}},\sx:\sA}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sB}}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}\vdash{}{\stfun{\sx}{\sA}{\st}}:{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sB}}\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st}:{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sB}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\ss}:{\sA}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\vdash{}{\st\,\ss}:{\sB}}

Figure 2.
\lambda_{G}
Typing Rules

We present the syntax of
\lambda_{G}
(pronounced “lambda gee” and
typeset in
blue sans-serif font
) in
Figure
1
, and the typing rules in
Figure
2
.
The language is call-by-value and includes standard type formers,
namely, the unit type
\sunitty
, product type
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}
, sum type

\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}
, and function type
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}
, with standard typing rules.
The language also includes some features specific to
gradual typing: a dynamic type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
, a dynamic type error
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}

and casts
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st
.
Following previous work, the interface for the dynamic type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}

is given by the casts themselves, and not distinct introduction and
elimination forms.
The values of the dynamic type are of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv
where
\stagty
ranges over
tag types
,
defined in Figure
1
.
The tag types are so called because they represent the “tags” used
to distinguish between the basic sorts of dynamically typed values.
Every type except
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
has an “underlying” tag type we write as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
and define in Figure
3
.
These tag types are the cases of the dynamic type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
seen as a
sum type, which is how we model it in §
3.2
.
For any two types
\sA,\sB
, we can form the cast
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}

which at runtime will attempt to coerce a term
\sv:\sA

into a valid term of type
\sB
.
If the value cannot sensibly be interpreted as a value in
\sB
, the
cast
fails
and reduces to the dynamic type error
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
.
The type error is like an uncatchable exception, modeling the fact
that the program crashes with an error message when a dynamic type
error is encountered. In this paper we consider all type errors to be
equivalent.
The calculus is based on that of
Wadler and
Findler 2009
, but does not have
blame and removes the restriction that types must be compatible in
order to define a cast.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\stagty

where
\sA\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sunitty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\sunitty\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\\
\end{array}

Figure 3.
\lambda_{G}
: Tag of a (non-dynamic) Type

9.24994pt\textsc{right=Fun}\par

Figure 4.
\lambda_{G}
Operational Semantics: non-casts (top) and
casts (bottom)

Figure
4
presents the operational semantics of the
gradual language in the style of
Felleisen and
Hieb 1992
, using

evaluation contexts

\sectxt
to specify a left-to-right,
call-by-value evaluation order.
The top of the figure shows the reductions
not
involving
casts.
This includes the standard reductions for pairs, sums, and functions
using the obvious notion of substitution
\st{}[\sgamma]
, in addition
to a reduction
\sectxt[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}}]\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
to propagate a dynamic
type error to the top level.

More importantly, the bottom of the figure shows the reductions of

casts
, specifying the dynamic type checking necessary for
gradual typing.
First (
DynDyn
), casting from dynamic to itself is the identity.
For any type
\sA
that is not a tag type (checked by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\neq\sA
) or the dynamic type, casting to the dynamic type first casts to
its underlying tag type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
and then tags it at that type (
TagUp
).
Similarly, casting down from the dynamic type first casts to the
underlying tag type (
TagDn
).
The next two rules are the primitive reductions for tags: if you project at
the correct tag type, you get the underlying value out (
TagMatch
) and otherwise a
dynamic type error is raised (
TagMismatch
).
Similarly, the next rule (
TagMismatch’
) says that if two
types are incompatible in that they have distinct tag types and
neither is dynamic, then the cast errors.
The next three (
Fun, Pair, Sum
) are the standard
“wrapping” implementations of contracts/casts
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
, also familiar from
subtyping.
For the function cast
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
,
note that while the output type is the same direction

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
, the input cast is flipped:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
.

We note that this standard operational semantics is quite complex for
such a
small
language.
In particular, it is more complicated than the operational semantics
of typed and dynamically typed languages of similar size.
Typed languages have reductions for each elimination form and
dynamically typed languages add only the possibility of type error to
those reductions.
Here on the other hand, the semantics is
not
modular in the
same way: there are five rules involving the dynamic type and four of them
involve comparing arbitrary types.

For these reasons, we find the cast calculus presentation inconvenient
for semantic analysis, and we choose not to develop our theory of
graduality or even prove type safety
directly
for this language.
Instead, we will
translate
the cast calculus into a typed
language where the casts are translated to functions implemented in
the language, i.e. contracts
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
.
This has the advantage of reducing the size of the language, making
“language-level” theorems like type safety and soundness of a
logical relation easier to prove.
Finally, note that our central theorems are still about the gradual
language, but we will prove them by lifting results about their
translations using an
adequacy
theorem (
theorem

3.8
).

3.
Translating Gradual Typing

We now translate our cast calculus into a simpler, non-gradual typed
language with errors.
We then prove an adequacy theorem that enables us to prove theorems
about gradual programs by reasoning about their translations.

3.1.
Typed Language with Errors

The typed language we will translate into is
\lambda_{T,\mho}

(pronounced “lambda tee error” and typeset in
bold
red serif font
),
a call-by-value typed lambda calculus with iso-recursive types and an
uncatchable error.
Figure
5
shows the syntax of the language.
Figure
6
shows some of the typing rules; the rest are
completely standard.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{Types}&\mty,\mtyalt&\mathrel{\bf::=}&\mmuty{\malpha}{\mty}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\malpha\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\munitty\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mB\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\msumty{\mty}{\mtyalt}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mfunty{\mty}{\mtyalt}\\
\mbox{Terms}&\mt,\ms&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mvar}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mtlet\mx\mt\ms\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtroll{\mA}{\mterm}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtunroll{\mterm}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mtunit\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mtpair\mt\ms\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mt}{\ms}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtinj{\mterm}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtinjpr{\mterm}}\\

&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\mtinj{\mectxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtinjpr{\mectxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtcase{\mectxt}{\mvarone}{\mtermone}{\mvartwo}{\mtermtwo}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtapp{\mectxt}{\mtermalt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtapp{\mval}{\mectxt}}\\
\mbox{Contexts}&\mctxt&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mtlet\mx\mC\ms\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mtlet\mx\mt\mC\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtroll{\mA}{\mctxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtunroll{\mctxt}}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\mtpair{\mctxt}{\mt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtpair{\mt}{\mctxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mctxt}{\mt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mt}{\mctxt}}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\mtinj{\mctxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtinjpr{\mctxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtcase{\mctxt}{\mvarone}{\mtermone}{\mvartwo}{\mtermtwo}}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\mtcase{\mterm}{\mvarone}{\mctxtone}{\mvartwo}{\mtermtwo}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtcase{\mterm}{\mvarone}{\mtermone}{\mvartwo}{\mctxttwo}}\\
&&\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}&{\mtfun{\mvar}{\mA}{\mctxt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtapp{\mctxt}{\mtermalt}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\mtapp{\mterm}{\mctxt}}\\
\mbox{Environments}&\menv&\mathrel{\bf::=}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\cdot}}}\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}{\menv},{\mvar}:{\mty}\\
\mbox{Substitutions}&\mgamma&\mathrel{\bf::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\bf\,\mid\,}\mgamma,\mv/\mx\end{array}

Figure 5.
\lambda_{T,\mho}
Syntax

\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mty

\frac{~}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\mathrel{:}\mty}\frac{\mvar:\mty\in\menv}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mvar\mathrel{:}\mty}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mA\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vdash}\ms\mathrel{:}\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\ms}\mathrel{:}\mB}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mterm\mathrel{:}\mA[\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}/\malpha]}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\mterm}\mathrel{:}\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mterm\mathrel{:}\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtunroll{\mterm}\mathrel{:}\mA[\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}/\malpha]}\frac{~}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtunit\mathrel{:}\munitty}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mA\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\ms\mathrel{:}\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtpair{\mt}{\ms}\mathrel{:}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mB}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mAtwo\menv,\mx:\mAone,\my:\mAtwo\mathrel{\vdash}\ms\mathrel{:}\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mt}{\ms}\mathrel{:}\mB}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mterm\mathrel{:}\mA}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtinj{\mterm}\mathrel{:}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mApr}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mterm\mathrel{:}\mApr}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtinjpr{\mterm}\mathrel{:}\msumty{\mA}{\mApr}}\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mterm\mathrel{:}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mApr\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vdash}\ms\mathrel{:}\mB\menv,\mxpr:\mApr\mathrel{\vdash}\mspr\mathrel{:}\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtcase{\mterm}{\mx}{\ms}{\mxpr}{\mspr}\mathrel{:}\mB}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mt}\mathrel{:}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\mathrel{:}\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\ms\mathrel{:}\mA}{\menv\mathrel{\vdash}\mt\,\ms\mathrel{:}\mB}

Figure 6.
\lambda_{T,\mho}
Typing Rules

The types of the language are similar to the cast calculus: they
include the standard type formers of products, sums, and functions.
Rather than the specific dynamic type, we include the more general,
but standard, iso-recursive type
\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}
,
which is isomorphic to the unfolding

\mA[\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}/\malpha]
by the terms

\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\cdot}
and
\mtunroll{\cdot}
.
As in the source language we have an uncatchable error
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
.

\frac{}{\mectxt[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\frac{}{\mectxt[{\mtlet\mx\mv\ms}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}\mectxt[{\ms{}[\mv/\mx]}]}\frac{}{\mectxt[{\mtunroll{(\mtroll{\mA}{\mval})}}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{1}}\mectxt[{\mval}]}\frac{}{\mectxt[{\mtmatchpair{\mxone}{\mxtwo}{\mtpair{\mvone}{\mvtwo}}{\mt}}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}\mectxt[{\mt[\mvone/\mxone,\mvtwo/\mxtwo]}]}\par\frac{}{\mectxt[{(\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mt})\,\mv}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}\mectxt[{\mt{}[\mv/\mx]}]}\frac{}{\mectxt[{\mtcase{({\mtinj{\mv}})}{\mx}{\mt}{\mxpr}{\mtpr}}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}\mectxt[{\mt{}[\mv/\mx]}]}\frac{}{\mectxt[{\mtcase{({\mtinjpr{\mv}})}{\mx}{\mt}{\mxpr}{\mtpr}}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}\mectxt[{\mtpr{}[\mv/\mxpr]}]}

\frac{~}{\mt\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mt}\frac{\mt\mathrel{\mapsto^{i}}\mtpr\mtpr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mtpr[2]}{\mt\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+j}}\mtpr[2]}

Figure 7.
\lambda_{T,\mho}
Operational Semantics

Figure
7
presents the operational semantics of
the language.
For the purposes of later defining a step-indexed logical relation, we
assign a weight to each small step of the operational semantics that
is
1
for unrolling a value of recursive type and
0
for other
reductions.
We then define a “quantitative” reflexive, transitive closure of
the small-step relation
\mt\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}\mtpr

that adds the weights of its constituent small steps.
When the number of steps is irrelevant, we just use
\mapsto
and

\mathrel{\Mapsto}
.
We can then establish some simple facts about this operational
semantics.

Lemma 3.1 (Subject Reduction).

If
\cdot\vdash\mt:\mA
and
\mt\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mtpr
then

\cdot\vdash\mtpr:\mA
.

Lemma 3.2 (Progress).

If
\cdot\vdash\mt:\mA
and
\mt
is not a value or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
,
then there exists
\mtpr
with
\mt\mapsto\mtpr
.

Proof.

By induction on the typing derivation for
\mt
.
∎

Lemma 3.3 (Determinism).

If
\mt\mapsto\ms
and
\mt\mapsto\mspr
, then
\ms=\mspr
.

3.2.
Translating Gradual Typing

Next we translate the cast calculus into our typed language, and prove
that the cast calculus semantics is in a simulation relation with the
typed language.
Since the two languages share so much of their syntax, most of the
translation is a simple “color change”, only the parts that are
truly components of gradual typing need much translation.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mmuty{\malpha}{\munitty\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}(\mpairty{\malpha}{\malpha})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}(\malpha\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\malpha)\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}(\mfunty{\malpha}{\malpha})}\\
\llbracket{}{\sunitty}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\munitty\\
\llbracket{}{\spairty{\sA}{\sB}}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mpairty{\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sB}\rrbracket{}}\\
\llbracket{}{\ssumty{\sA}{\sB}}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\msumty{\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sB}\rrbracket{}}\\
\llbracket{}{\sfunty{\sA}{\sB}}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mfunty{\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sB}\rrbracket{}}\\
\end{array}

Figure 8.
Type Translation

Our translation is type preserving, so we first define a
type
translation
in Figure
8
.
The dynamic type is interpreted as a recursive sum of the translations
of the tag types of the gradual language.
The unit, pair, sum and function types are all interpreted as the
corresponding connectives in the typed language.

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}

where if
~\sxone:\sAone,\ldots,\sxn:\sAn\vdash\st:\sA~
then

~\mxone:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{},\ldots,\mxn:\llbracket{}{\sAn}\rrbracket{}\vdash\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket{}{\svar}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mvar\\
\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}]\\
\llbracket{}{\stunit}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtunit\\

\llbracket{}{\stinj\st}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtinj{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}\\
\llbracket{}{\stinjpr\st}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtinjpr{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}\\
\llbracket{}{\stcase{\st}{\sx}{\ss}{\sxpr}{\sspr}}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtcase{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}{\mx}{\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket{}}{\mxpr}{\llbracket{}{\sspr}\rrbracket{}}\\

\llbracket{}{\st\,\ss}\rrbracket&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\,\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket\\

Direct Cast Translation

Figure 11.
Functorial Action of Type Connectives

Next, we define the translation of terms in
Figure
9
, which is type preserving in that if

\sxone:\sAone,\ldots,\sxn:\sAn\vdash\st:\sA
then

\mxone:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{},\ldots,\mxn:\llbracket{}{\sAn}\rrbracket{}\vdash\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}
.
Again, most of the translation is just a change of hue.
The most important rule of the term translation is that of casts.
A cast
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
is translated to an
evaluation context

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}
of the appropriate type, which are defined
in Figure
10
.
Each case of the definition corresponds to one or more rules of the
operational semantics.
The product, sum, and function rules use the definitions of functorial
actions of their types from Figure
11
.
We separate them because we will use the functoriality property in
several definitions, theorems, and proofs later.

3.3.
Operational Properties

Next, we consider the relationship between the operational semantics
of the two languages and how to lift properties of the typed language
to the gradual language.
We want to view the translation of the cast calculus into the typed
language as
definitional
, and in that regard view the
operational semantics of the source language as being based on the
typed language. We capture this relationship in the following
forward

simulation theorem, which says that any reduction in the cast calculus
corresponds to (and is
justified by
) multiple steps in the target:

Lemma 3.4 (Translation Preserves Values, Evaluation Contexts).

(1)

For any value
\sv
,
\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}
is a value.

(2)

For any evaluation context
\sectxt
,
\llbracket{}{\sectxt}\rrbracket{}
is an
evaluation context.

Lemma 3.5 (Simulation of Operational Semantics).

If
\st\mapsto\stpr
then there exists
\ms
with
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mapsto\ms
and
\ms\mathrel{\Mapsto}\llbracket{}{\stpr}\rrbracket{}
.

Proof.

By cases of
\st\mapsto\stpr
. The non-cast cases are clear by

lemma

3.4
.

(1)

DynDyn

\displaystyle\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}]

\displaystyle=\mtlet\mx{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}\mx

\displaystyle\mapsto\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}

(2)

TagUp: Trivial because
\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}
.

(3)

TagDn: Trivial because
\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}
.

(4)

(TagMatch) Valid because

\displaystyle\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}}{\stagty}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mtelsecasevert{{\mtsum{\stagty}{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}}{\stagty}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}

(5)

(TagMismatch) Valid because

\displaystyle\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagtypr}{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}}{\stagty}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mtelsecasevert{{\mtsum{\stagtypr}{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}}{\stagty}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}}\rrbracket{}

(6)

(TagMismatch’) Valid because

\mtlet\mx{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}

(7)

Pair Valid by

\displaystyle\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\svpr}\rrbracket{}}}]

\displaystyle=\mtlet{\mx}{\my}{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\svpr}\rrbracket{}}}{\mtpair{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mx}]}{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\my}]}}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mtpair{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\svpr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{}{\stpair{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{\sv}}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{\svpr}}}}\rrbracket{}

(8)

Sum

\displaystyle\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mtinj{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}]

\displaystyle=\mtcasevert{\mtinj{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}{\mx}{\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mx}]}{\mxpr}{\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mxpr}]}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}]

\displaystyle=\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}

(9)

Sum’

\displaystyle\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mtinjpr{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}]

\displaystyle=\mtcasevert{\mtinjpr{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}}{\mx}{\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mx}]}{\mxpr}{\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mxpr}]}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}]

\displaystyle=\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}\rrbracket{}

(10)

(Fun) Valid because

\displaystyle\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}]

\displaystyle\mapsto\mtletvert{\mxin f}{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}}{}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mtfun{\mxin a}{\mAtwo}{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}\,(\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mxin a}])}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{}{\stfun{\sxin a}{\sAtwo}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{({\sv}\,({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\sxin{a}}}))}}}\rrbracket{}

∎

To lift theorems for the gradual language from the typed language, we
need to establish an
adequacy
theorem, which says
that the operational behavior of a translated term determines the
behavior of the original source term.
To do this we use the following backward simulation theorem.

Lemma 3.6 (Translation reflects Results).

(1)

If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is a value,
\st\mapsto^{*}\sv
for some
\sv

with
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}
.

(2)

If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, then
\st\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\st
. For the non-casts, follows by inductive
hypothesis. For the casts, only two cases can be values:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st
: if
\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is a value then by inductive hypothesis,
\st
is a
value, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv\mapsto\sv
.

(2)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st
: if

\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}}
is a value, then

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is a value so by inductive hypothesis
\st\mapsto^{*}\sv
so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv
.

For the error case, there is only one case where it is possible for

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
without
\st={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
:

(1)

For
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\ss
, if

\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\rrbracket{}\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket{}
is an error then
clearly
\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket{}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
so by inductive hypothesis
\ss\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}

and because casts are strict,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\ss\mapsto^{*}\ss

∎

Lemma 3.7 (Backward Simulation).

If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mapsto\ms
then there exists
\sspr
with
\st\mapsto\sspr
and
\ms\mathrel{\Mapsto}\llbracket{}{\sspr}\rrbracket{}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\st
. We show two illustrative cases, the rest
follow by the same reasoning.

(1)

\llbracket{}{\stmatchpair{\sx}{\sy}{\st}{\ss}}\rrbracket{}=\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\ss}\rrbracket{}}
. If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is not a
value, then we use the inductive hypothesis. If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is a
value and
\st
is then by Lemma
3.6

\st\mapsto^{*}\sv
and then we can reduce the pattern-match in source
and target.

(2)

\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st}\rrbracket{}=(\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}})[{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}]
. If
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
is
not a value, we use the inductive hypothesis. Otherwise, if it is
a value and
\st
is not, we use Lemma
3.6
to get

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st}\mapsto^{*}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sv}
. Then
we use the same argument as the proof of
Lemma
3.5
.

(3)

\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st}\rrbracket{}=\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}}]}]

then we use the same argument as the case for

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\st
, e.g., the function case above.

∎

Theorem 3.8 (Adequacy).

(1)

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mv
if and only if
\st\mapsto^{*}\sv
with

\llbracket{}{\sv}\rrbracket{}=\mv
.

(2)

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
if and only if
\st\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
.

(3)

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
diverges if and only if
\st
diverges

Proof.

The forward direction for values and errors is given by forward
simulation Lemma
3.5
. The backward direction for values
and errors is given by induction on
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mtpr
,
backward simulation Lemma
3.7
and reflection of results

lemma

3.6
.

If
\st
diverges, then by the backward value and error cases, it
follows that
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
does not run to a value or error. By type safety
of the typed language,
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
diverges.

Finally, if
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}
diverges, we show that
\st
diverges. If

\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mapsto\ms
, then by backward simulation, there exists

\sspr
with
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mapsto\sspr
and
\ms\mathrel{\Mapsto}\llbracket{}{\sspr}\rrbracket{}
. Since
\llbracket{}{\st}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\llbracket{}{\sspr}\rrbracket{}
, we know

\llbracket{}{\sspr}\rrbracket{}
diverges, so by coinduction
\sspr
diverges and
therefore
\st
diverges.
∎

While this has reduced the number of primitives of the language,
reasoning about the behavior of the translated casts isn’t any simpler
than the original operational semantics since they have the same
behavior.
For simpler reasoning about cast behavior, we will move further away
from a direct simulation of the source operational semantics, to a
second semantics based on ep pairs that is observationally equivalent
but also conceptually simpler and helps prove the gradual guarantee.
However, in order to prove that the second semantics is equivalent, we
first need to develop a usable theory of observational equivalence and approximation.

4.
A Logical Relation for Error Approximation

Next, we define observational equivalence and error approximation of
programs in the gradual and typed languages, the two properties with
which we formulate embedding-projection pairs.
To facilitate proofs of error approximation, we develop a novel step-indexed
logical relation. Since our notion of approximation is non-standard, the use of
step-indexing in our logical relation is inconvenient to use directly.
So, on top of the “implementation” of the logical relation as a
step-indexed relation, we prove many high-level lemmas so that all
proofs in the next sections are performed relative to these lemmas,
and none manipulate step indices directly.

4.1.
Observational Equivalence and Approximation

A suitable notion of equivalence for programs is
observational
equivalence
.
We say
\st
is observationally equivalent to
\stpr
if replacing one
with the other in the context of a larger program produces the same
result (termination, error, or divergence).
We formalize this saying a program
context

\sC
is a term
with a single hole
[\cdot]
.
A context is typed
\senvpr\vdash\sC[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vdash\cdot:\sA}]:\sApr
when for any term
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vdash\st:\sA
, replacing the hole
with
\st
results in a well-typed
\senvpr\vdash\sC[{\st}]:\sApr

While this notion of observational equivalence is entirely standard,
the notion of
approximation
we use—which we call error
approximation—is not the standard notion of observational
approximation.
Usually, we would say
\st
observationally approximates
\stpr
if, when
placing them into the same context
\sC
, either
\sC[\st]
diverges or
they both terminate or both error.
We call this form of approximation
divergence approximation
.
However, for
gradual typing
we are not particularly interested
in when one program diverges more than another, but rather when it
produces more
type errors
.
We might be tempted to conflate the two, but their behavior is quite
distinct!
We can never truly know if a black-box program will continue
indefinitely: it would frustrate any programmer to use a language that
runs forever when accidentally using a function as a number.
The reader should keep this difference in mind when seeing how our
logical relation differs form the standard treatment.
In the rest of this paper, when discussing the two together we will
clearly distinguish between divergence and error approximation, but
when there is no qualifier,
approximation is meant as
error approximation
.

Definition 4.1 (Gradual Observational Equivalence, Error
Approximation).

For any well typed terms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vdash\st,\stpr:\sA
,

(1)

Define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\st\approx^{\text{obs}}\stpr:\sA
, pronounced
“
\st
is observationally equivalent to
\stpr
” to hold when for
any
\cdot\vdash\sC[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vdash\cdot:\sA}]:\sB
, either
\sC[\st]
and

\sC[\stpr]
both reduce to a value, both reduce to an error, or
both diverge.

(2)

Define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\st\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\stpr:\sA
, pronounced
“
\st
observationally (error) approximates
\stpr
” to hold
when for any
\cdot\vdash\sC[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vdash\cdot:\sA}]:\sB
, either
\sC[\st]
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}
or both
\sC[\st]

and
\sC[\stpr]
reduce to a value or both diverge.

As with divergence approximation, we can prove two programs
are observationally equivalent by showing each error approximates the other.

Lemma 4.2 (Equivalence is Approximation Both Ways).

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\stone\approx^{\text{obs}}\sttwo:\sA
if and only if both

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\stone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\sttwo:\sA
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\sttwo\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\stone:\sA
.

We define typed observational equivalence
\menv\vDash\mt\approx^{\text{obs}}\mtpr:\mA
and observational error approximation
\menv\vDash\mt\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\mtpr:\mA
with the exact same definition as for the
gradual language above, but in
red
instead of

blue
.
We rarely work with the gradual language directly, instead we prove
approximation results for their translations.

Definition 4.3 (Typed Observational equivalence, Error Approximation).

For any well typed terms
\menv\vdash\mt,\mtpr:\mA
,

(1)

Define
\menv\vDash\mt\approx^{\text{obs}}\mtpr:\mA
, pronounced
“
\mt
is observationally equivalent to
\mtpr
” to hold when for
any
\mC[\cdot:\mA]:\sB
, either
\mC[\mt]
and

\mC[\mtpr]
both reduce to a value, both reduce to an error, or
both diverge.

(2)

Define
\menv\vDash\mt\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\mtpr:\mA
, pronounced
“
\mt
observationally (error) approximates to
\mtpr
” to hold when
for any
\mC[\cdot:\mA]:\sB
, either
\mC[\mt]
reduces to

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
or both
\mC[\mt]
and
\mC[\mtpr]
reduce to a value or
both diverge.

This is justified by the following lemma, a consequence of our

adequacy
result (
theorem

3.8
).

Lemma 4.4 (Typed Observational Approximation implies Gradual Observational Approximation).

If
\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\rrbracket{}\vDash\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\vDash\stone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\sttwo:\sA

Proof.

For any
\sC
, by compositionality of the translation,

\llbracket{}{\sC[\stone]}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{\mC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}]
and
\llbracket{}{\sC[\sttwo]}\rrbracket{}=\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}]
.
Then we analyze
\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}

(1)

If
\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}}
then
theorem

3.8

states that
\sC[\stone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mho}}}}
.

(2)

If
\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}]
diverges, then
\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}]

also diverges and therefore by
theorem

3.8
,
\sC[\stone]

and
\sC[\sttwo]
diverge.

(3)

If
\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\svone
, then

\llbracket{}{\sC}\rrbracket{}[\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\svtwo
and therefore by

theorem

3.8
,
\sC[\stone]\mapsto^{*}\svone
and

\sC[\sttwo]\mapsto^{*}\svtwo
.

∎

4.2.
Logical Relation

Observational equivalence and approximation are extremely difficult to
prove directly, so we use the usual method of proving observational
results by using a
logical relation
that we prove
sound

with respect to observational approximation.
Due to the non-well-founded nature of recursive types (and the dynamic
type specifically), we develop a
step-indexed
logical relation
following
Ahmed 2006
.
We define our logical relation for error approximation in
Figure
12
.
Because our notion of error approximation is not the standard notion
of approximation, the definition is a bit unusual, but this is
necessary for technical reasons.

It is key to compositional reasoning about embedding-projection pairs
that approximation be
transitive
and care must be taken to show
transitivity for a step-indexed relation.
However, for standard definitions of logical relations for
observational equivalence, it is difficult to prove
transitivity directly. Therefore, it is often established through indirect
reasoning—e.g., by setting up a biorthogonal (
\top\top
-closed)
logical relation so one can easily show it is complete with respect to
observational equivalence, which in turn implies that it must be
transitive since observational equivalence is easily proven transitive.
The reason establishing transitivity is tricky is that a step-indexed relation
is
not
transitive at a
fixed
index, i.e., if
e_{1}\mathrel{\sim^{i}}e_{2}
and

e_{2}\mathrel{\sim^{i}}e_{3}
it is not necessarily the case that
e_{1}\mathrel{\sim^{i}}e_{3}
. For instance,
e_{1}\mathrel{\sim^{i}}e_{2}
might be related because
e_{1}
terminates
in less than
i
steps and has the same behavior as
e_{2}
which takes more than

i
steps to terminate, whereas
e_{2}\mathrel{\sim^{i}}e_{3}
are related because they both
take
i
steps of reduction so cannot be distinguished in
i
steps but have
different behavior when run for more steps.
One direct method for proving transitivity, originally presented in

Ahmed 2006
, is to observe that two terms are observationally
equivalent when each divergence approximates the other, and then use a
step-indexed relation for divergence approximation.
Because a conjunction of transitive relations is transitive, this
proves transitivity of equivalence.
A step-indexed relation for divergence approximation can be shown to
have a kind of “half-indexed” transitivity, i.e., if
e_{1}\mathrel{\prec^{i}}e_{2}
and for every natural
j
, we know
e_{2}\mathrel{\prec^{j}}e_{3}
then
e_{1}\mathrel{\prec^{i}}e_{3}
.
We have a similar issue with error approximation: the naïve logical
relation for error approximation is not clearly transitive.
Inspired by the case of observational equivalence, we similarly
“split” our logical relation into two relations that can be proven
transitive by an argument similar to divergence approximation.
However, unlike for observational equivalence, our two relations are not the
same. Instead, one
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}
is error approximation up to divergence on the

left
and the other
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}
is error approximation up to
divergence on the
right
.

For a given natural number
i\in\mathbb{N}
and type
\mA
, and

closed terms

\mtone,\mttwo
of type
\mA
,
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
intuitively means that, if we only inspect

\mtone
’s behavior up to
i
uses of
\mtunroll\cdot
, then it
appears that
\mtone
error approximates
\mttwo
.
Less constructively, it means that we cannot show that
\mtone
does

not
error approximate
\mttwo
when limited to
i
uses of

\mtunroll\cdot
.
However, even if we knew
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
for

every

i\in\mathbb{N}
, it still might be the case that

\mtone
diverges, since no finite number of unrolling can ever
exhaust
\mtone
’s behavior.
So we also require that we know
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
,
which means that up to
i
uses of unroll on
\mttwo
, it appears that

\mtone
error approximates
\mttwo
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}},\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}&\subseteq&\{\mt\mathrel{|}\cdot\vdash\mt:\mA\}^{2}\\
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(\exists\mtonepr.~\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}\mtonepr)\\
&&\vee(\exists j\leq i.~\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}})\\
&&\vee(\exists j\leq i,\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo.~\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mvone\wedge\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvtwo)\\
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(\exists\mttwopr.~\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}\mttwopr)\\
&&\vee(\exists j\leq i.~\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\wedge\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}})\\
&&\vee(\exists j\leq i,\mvtwo.~\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mvtwo\wedge\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt(\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\vee\exists\mvone.~\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvone\wedge\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i-j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo)\\
\\
\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\mA}},\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}&\subseteq&\{\mv\mathrel{|}\cdot\vdash\mv:\mA\}^{2}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where }{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}\in\{{\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\cdot}_{\cdot,\cdot}}},{\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{\cdot}_{\cdot,\cdot}}}\}\\
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{0}_{v,\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}}\mvtwo&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\top\\
\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\mvone}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i+1}_{v,\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}}\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\mvtwo}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mA[\malpha\mapsto\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}]}}\mvtwo\\
\mtunit\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\munitty}}\mtunit&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\top\\
\mtpair{\mvone}{\mvonepr}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mpairty{\mA}{\mApr}}}\mtpair{\mvtwo}{\mvtwopr}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo\wedge\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mApr}}\mvtwopr\\
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\msumty{\mA}{\mB}}}\mvtwo&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(\exists(\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwopr)\wedge\mvone=\mtinj{\mvonepr}\wedge\mvtwo=\mtinj{\mvtwopr})\\
&&\vee(\exists(\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mB}}\mvtwopr)\wedge\mvone=\mtinjpr{\mvonepr}\wedge\mvtwo=\mtinjpr{\mvtwopr})\\
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mfunty{\mA}{\mB}}}\mvtwo&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\forall j\leq i.\forall(\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwopr).~\mtapp{\mvone}{\mvonepr}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mB}}\mtapp{\mvtwo}{\mvtwopr}\\
\\
\cdot\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\cdot}}\cdot&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\top\\
\mgammain{1},\mvin{1}/\mx\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\menv,\mvar:\mA}}\mgammain{2},\mvin{2}/\mx&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mgammain{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\menv}}\mgammain{2}\wedge\mvin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvin{2}\\
\\
\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}\mttwo:\mA&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\forall i\in\mathbb{N},(\mgammain{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\menv}}\mgammain{2}).~\mtone[\mgammain{1}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo[\mgammain{2}]\\
\\
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\menv\vdash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}\mttwo:\mA\wedge\menv\vdash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}\mttwo:\mA\end{array}

Figure 12.
\lambda_{T,\mho}
Error Approximation Logical Relation

The above intuition should help to understand the definition of error
approximation for terms (i.e., the relations
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}_{t}
and
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}_{t}
).
The relation
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
is defined by inspection
of
\mtone
’s behavior: it holds if
\mtone
is still running after

i+1
unrolls; or if it steps to an error in fewer than
i
unrolls; or
if it results in a value in fewer than
i
unrolls and also
\mttwo

runs to a value and those values are related for the remaining steps.
The definition of
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
is
defined by inspection of
\mttwo
’s behavior: it holds if
\mttwo
is
still running after
i+1
unrolls; or if
\mttwo
steps to an error in
fewer than
i
steps then
\mtone
errors as well; or if
\mttwo

steps to a value, either
\mtone
errors or steps to a value
related for the remaining steps.

While the relations and
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}_{t}
on terms are different, fortunately, the relations on
values are essentially the same, so we abstract over the cases by
having the symbol
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}
to range over either
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}
or

\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}
.
For values of recursive type, if the step-index is
0
, we consider
them related, because otherwise we would need to perform an unroll to
inspect them further.
Otherwise, we decrement the index and check if they are related.
Decrementing the index here is exactly what makes the definition of
the relation well-founded.
For the standard types, the value relation definition is indeed
standard: pairs are related when the two sides are related, sums must
be the same case and functions must be related when applied to any
related values in the future (i.e., when we may have exhausted some of
the available steps).

Finally, we extend these relations to
open
terms in the
