standard way: we define substitutions to be related pointwise (similar
to products) and then say that
\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}\mttwo:\mA
holds if for every pair of substitutions
\mgammaone,\mgammatwo

related for
i
steps, the terms after substitution, written

\mtone[\mgammaone]
and
\mttwo[\mgammatwo]
, are related for
i
steps.
Then our resulting relation
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo
is
defined to hold when
\mtone
error approximates
\mttwo
up to
divergence of
\mtone
(
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}
),
and
up to divergence of

\mttwo
(
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}
).

\frac{~}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}:\mty}\par\frac{\mvar:\mty\in\menv}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mvar\sqsubseteq\mvar:\mty}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vDash}\msone\sqsubseteq\mstwo:\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtlet{\mx}{\mtone}{\msone}\sqsubseteq\mtlet{\mx}{\mttwo}{\mstwo}:\mB}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtermone\sqsubseteq\mtermtwo:\mA[\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}/\malpha]}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\mtermone}\sqsubseteq\mtroll{\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\mtermtwo}:\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtermone\sqsubseteq\mtermtwo:\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtunroll{\mtermone}\sqsubseteq\mtunroll{\mtermtwo}:\mA[\mmuty{\malpha}{\mA}/\malpha]}\par\frac{~}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtunit\sqsubseteq\mtunit:\munitty}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\msone\sqsubseteq\mstwo:\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtpair{\mtone}{\msone}\sqsubseteq\mtpair{\mttwo}{\mstwo}:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mB}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mAtwo\menv,\mx:\mAone,\my:\mAtwo\mathrel{\vDash}\msone\sqsubseteq\mstwo:\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mtone}{\msone}\sqsubseteq\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mttwo}{\mstwo}:\mB}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtermone\sqsubseteq\mtermtwo:\mA}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtinj{\mtermone}\sqsubseteq\mtinj{\mtermtwo}:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mApr}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtermone\sqsubseteq\mtermtwo:\mApr}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtinjpr{\mtermone}\sqsubseteq\mtinjpr{\mtermtwo}:\msumty{\mA}{\mApr}}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtermone\sqsubseteq\mtermtwo:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mApr\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vDash}\msone\sqsubseteq\mstwo:\mB\menv,\mxpr:\mApr\mathrel{\vDash}\msonepr\sqsubseteq\mstwopr:\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtcase{\mtermone}{\mx}{\msone}{\mxpr}{\msonepr}\sqsubseteq\mtcase{\mtermtwo}{\mx}{\mstwo}{\mxpr}{\mstwopr}:\mB}\par\frac{\menv,\mx:\mA\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mB}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtufun{\mx}{\mtone}\sqsubseteq\mtufun{\mx}{\mttwo}:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB}\par\frac{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\sqsubseteq\mtone:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\msone\sqsubseteq\mstwo:\mA}{\menv\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\,\msone\sqsubseteq\mttwo\,\mstwo:\mB}

Figure 13.
\lambda_{T,\mho}
Error Approximation Congruence Rules

We need the following standard lemmas.

Lemma 4.5 (Downward Closure).

If
j\leq i
then

(1)

If
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
then
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo

(2)

If
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
then
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
.

Proof.

By lexicographic induction on the pair
(i,\mA)
.
∎

Lemma 4.6 (Anti-Reduction).

This theorem is different for the two relations as we allow arbitrary
steps on the “divergence greater-than” side.

(1)

If
\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}^{i}_{t,\mA}\mttwo
and
\mtonepr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mtone
and
\mttwopr\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mttwo
then

\mtonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}^{i+j}_{t,\mA}\mttwopr
.

(2)

If
\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}^{i}_{t,\mA}\mttwo
and
\mttwopr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mttwo
and
\mtonepr\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mtone
, then

\mtonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}^{i+j}_{t,\mA}\mttwopr
.

A simple corollary that applies in common cases to both relations is
that if
\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}^{i}_{t,\mA}\mttwo
and
\mtonepr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mtone
and
\mttwopr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mttwo
, then

\mtonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}^{i}_{t,\mA}\mttwopr
.

Proof.

By direct inspection and downward closure
(Lemma
4.5
).
∎

Lemma 4.7 (Monadic Bind).

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, if for any
j\leq i
and
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{t,\mA}}\mvtwo
, we can show
\mectxtone[{\mvone}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{t,\mA}}\mectxttwo[{\mvtwo}]
holds, then for any
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
, it is the case that

\mectxtone[{\mvone}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mectxttwo[{\mvtwo}]
.

Proof.

We consider the proof for
\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}
, the other is
similar/easier. By case analysis of
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
.

(1)

If
\mttwo
takes
i+1
steps, so does
\mectxttwo[{\mttwo}]
.

(2)

If
\mttwo\mathrel{\mapsto^{j\leq i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
and
\mtone\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, then first of all
\mectxttwo[{\mttwo}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{j}}\mectxttwo[{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}]\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
. If
j+1=i
, we are
done. Otherwise
\mectxttwo[{\mttwo}]\mathrel{\mapsto^{j+1\leq i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}

and
\mectxtone[{\mtone}]\mapsto^{*}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
.

(3)

Assume there exist
j\leq i
,
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i-j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
and
\mttwo\mathrel{\mapsto^{j}}\mvtwo
and
\mtone\mapsto^{*}\mvone
.
Then by assumption,
\mectxtone[{\mvone}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i-j}_{t,\mectxttwo}}[{\mvtwo}]
. Then by antireduction
(Lemma
4.6
),

\mectxtone[{\mtone}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mectxttwo}}[{\mttwo}]
.

∎

We then prove that our logical relation is sound for observational
error approximation by showing that it is a congruence relation and showing that if
we can prove error approximation up to divergence on the
left
and
on the right, then we have true error approximation.

Lemma 4.8 (Congruence for Logical Relation).

All of the congruence rules in Figure
13
are valid.

Proof.

Each case is done by proving the implication for
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}^{i}
and

\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}^{i}
. Most cases follow by monadic bind
(Lemma
4.7
), downward closure
(Lemma
4.5
) and direct use of the inductive
hypotheses.
We show some illustrative cases.

(1)

Given
\mgammaone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}{i}{\menv}\mgammatwo
, we need to
show
\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mtone[\mgammaone]}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{i}_{t,\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB}}\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mttwo[\mgammatwo]}
. Since they are values, we
show they are related values. Given any
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\sim}}^{j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
with
j\leq i
, each side
\beta
reduces in
0
unroll
steps so it is sufficient to show

\mtone[\mgammaone,\mvonepr/\mx]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{j}_{t,\mB}}\mttwo[\mgammatwo,\mvtwopr/\mx]

Which follows by inductive hypothesis and downward-closure and the
substitution relation.

∎

Theorem 4.9 (Logical Relation implies Observational Error Approximation).

If
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA
, then
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\mttwo:\mA

Proof.

If
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA
, then for any closing
context, by
lemma

4.8
,
\cdot\vDash\mC[\mtone]\sqsubseteq\mC[\mttwo]:\sB
holds.

Then we do a case analysis of
\mC[\mtone]
’s behavior.

(1)

If
\mC[\mtone]
diverges, then for any
i
, since

\mC[\mtone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\sB}}\mC[\mttwo]
, only the

\mC[\mttwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}\mtpr
is possible, so
\mC[\mttwo]
also
diverges.

(2)

If
\mC[\mtone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
we’re done.

(3)

If
\mC[\mtone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}\mtunit
, then because

\mC[\mtone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\sB}}\mC[\mttwo]
, we know

\mC[\mttwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mtunit
.

∎

4.3.
Approximation and Equivalence Lemmas

The step-indexed logical relation is on the face of it quite complex,
especially due to the splitting of error approximation into two
step-indexed relations.
However, we should view the step-indexed relation as an
“implementation” of the high-level concept of error approximation,
and we work as much as possible with the error
approximation relation
\menv\vDash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA
.
In order to do this we now prove some high-level lemmas, which are
proven using the step-indexed relations, but allow us to develop
conceptual proofs of the key theorems of the paper.

First, there is reflexivity, also known as the
fundamental
lemma
, which is proved using the same congruence cases as the
soundness theorem (
theorem

4.9
.)
Note that by the definition of our logical relation, this is really a
kind of
monotonicity
theorem for every term in the language,
the first component of our graduality proof.

Corollary 4.10 (Reflexivity).

If
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
then
\menv\vDash\mt\sqsubseteq\mt:\mA

Proof.

By induction on the typing derivation of
\mt
, in each case using
the corresponding congruence rule from Lemma
4.8
.∎

Next, crucial to reasoning about ep pairs is the use of

transitivity
, a notoriously tedious property to prove for
step-indexed logical relations.
This is where our splitting of error-approximation into two pieces
proves essential, adapting the approach for divergence-approximation
relations introduced in
Ahmed 2006
.
The proof works as follows: due to the function and open-term cases,
we cannot simply prove transitivity in the limit directly.
Instead we get a kind of “asymmetric” transitivity: if
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
and
for any

j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mttwo\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{j}_{t,\mA}}\mtthree
, then we know
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mtthree
. We abbreviate the
\forall j
part as
\mttwo\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mtthree
in what follows.
The key to the proof is in the function and open terms cases, which
rely on reflexivity,
corollary

4.10
, as in

Ahmed 2006
. Reflexivity says that when we have
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
then we also have
\mvtwo\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
, which allows us to use the inductive
hypothesis.

Lemma 4.11 (Transitivity for Closed Terms/Values).

The following are true for any
\mA
.

(1)

If
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
and
\mtin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}
then
\mtin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}
.

(2)

If
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mvtwo
and
\mvin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mvin{3}
then
\mvin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mvin{3}
.

Similarly,

(1)

If
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
and

\mtin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}

then
\mtin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}
.

(2)

If
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mvtwo
and

\mvin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mvin{3}
then

\mvin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mvin{3}
.

Proof.

We prove the
\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}
and
\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}
mutually by
induction on
(i,\mA)
. The other logical relation is similar. Most
value cases are simple uses of the inductive hypotheses.

(1)

(Terms) By case analysis of
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
.

(a)

If
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}\mtonepr
or
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, we have the result.

(b)

Let
j\leq i
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo)
with
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mvone
and

\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k}}\mvtwo
. By inductive hypothesis for values,
it is sufficient to show that
\mtin{3}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvin{3}

and
\mvtwo\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{v,\mA}}\mvin{3}
.

Since
\mtin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}
, in particular
we know
\mtin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{k+l}_{t,\mA}}\mtin{3}
for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
, so since
\mtin{2}\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k}}\mvin{2}
, we
know that
\mtin{3}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvin{3}
and
\mvin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{l}_{v,\mA}}\mvin{3}
, for every
l
, i.e.,
\mvin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{v,\mA}}\mvin{3}
.

(2)

(Function values) Suppose
\mvin{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\mfunty{\mA}{\mB}}}\mvin{2}
and
\mvin{2}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{v,\mfunty{\mA}{\mB}}}\mvin{3}
. Then, let
j\leq i
and
\mvmetavar{1}{}{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{j}_{v,\mA}}\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}
. We need to show

\mtapp{\mvin{1}}{\mvmetavar{1}{}{1}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{j}_{t,\mB}}\mtapp{\mvin{3}}{\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}}
. By inductive hypothesis, it is
sufficient to show
\mtapp{\mvin{1}}{\mvmetavar{1}{}{1}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{j}_{t,\mB}}\mtapp{\mvin{2}}{\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}}
and
\mtapp{\mvin{2}}{\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{t,\mB}}\mtapp{\mvin{3}}{\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}}
.

The former is clear. The latter follows by the congruence rule for
application Lemma
4.8
and reflexivity

corollary

4.10
on
\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}
: since
\cdot\vdash\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}:\mA
, we have
\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{\omega}_{v,\mA}}\mvmetavar{2}{}{1}
.

∎

Lemma 4.12 (Transitivity).

If
\menv\vdash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mttwo:\mA
and
\menv\vdash\mttwo\sqsubseteq\mtin{3}:\mA
then
\menv\vdash\mtone\sqsubseteq\mtin{3}:\mA
.

Proof.

The argument is essentially the same as the function value case,
invoking the fundamental lemma
corollary

4.10
for each
component of the substitutions and transitivity for the closed
relation Lemma
4.11

∎

Next, we want to extract approximation and equivalence principles for

open
programs from syntactic operational properties of

closed
programs.
First, obviously any operational reduction is a contextual
equivalence, and the next lemma extends that to open programs.
Note that we use
\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}
to mean approximation in both directions,
i.e., equivalence:

Lemma 4.13 (Open
\beta
Reductions).

Given
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
,
\menv\vdash\mtpr:\mA
, if for
every
\mgamma:\Gamma
,
\mt[\mgamma]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mtpr{}[\mgamma]
,
then
\menv\vDash\mt\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtpr:\mA
.

Proof.

By reflexivity
corollary

4.10
on
\mtpr,\mgamma

and anti-reduction
lemma

4.6
.
∎

We call this open
\beta
reduction because we will use it to justify
equivalences that look like an operational reduction, but have open
values (i.e. including variables) rather than closed as in the
operational semantics.
For instance,

\mtlet{\mx}{\my}{\mt}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mt[\my/\mx]

and

\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mtpair{\mxpr}{\mypr}}{\mt}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mt[\mxpr/\mx,\mypr/\my]

Additionally, it is convenient to use
\eta
expansions for our
types as well.
Note that since we are using a call-by-value language, the
\eta

expansion for functions is restricted to
values
.

Lemma 4.14 (
\eta
Expansion).

(1)

For any
\menv\vdash\mv:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mB
,
\mv\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mv\,\mx}

(2)

For any
\menv,\mx:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mApr,\menvpr\vdash\mt:\mB
,

\mt\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcase{\mx}{\my}{\mt[\mtinj\mypr/\mx]}{\mypr}{\mt[\mtinjpr\mypr/\mx]}

(3)

For any
\menv,\mx:\mA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mApr,\menvpr\vdash\mt:\mB
,

\mt\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpair{\my}{\mypr}{\mx}{\mt[\mtpair{\my}{\mypr}/\mx]}

Proof.

All are consequences of
lemma

4.13
.
∎

Next, with term constructors that involve continuations, we often need
to rearrange the programs such as the “case-of-case” transformation.
These are called commuting conversions and are presented in Figure
14
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mectxt[{\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\ms}}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\mectxt[{\ms}]}\\
\mectxt[{\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mt}{\ms}}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&\mtmatchpair{\mx}{\my}{\mt}{\mectxt[{\ms}]}\\
\mectxt[{\mtcase{\mt}{\mx}{\ms}{\mxpr}{\mspr}}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&\mtcase{\mt}{\mx}{\mectxt[{\ms}]}{\mxpr}{\mectxt[{\mspr}]}\end{array}

Figure 14.
Commuting Conversions

Lemma 4.15 (Commuting Conversions).

All of the commuting conversions in Figure
14
are
equivalences.

Proof.

By monadic bind, anti-reduction and the reflexivity
(
lemmas

4.7
,
4.6
and
4.10
).
∎

Next, the following theorem is the main reason we so heavily use

evaluation contexts
.
It is a kind of open version of the monadic bind lemma

lemma

4.7
.

Lemma 4.16 (Evaluation contexts are linear).

If
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
and
\menv,\mx:\mA\vdash\mectxt[{\mx}]:\mB
, then

\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\mectxt[{\mx}]}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mectxt[{\mt}]

Proof.

By a commuting conversion and an open
\beta
reduction, connected
by transitivity
lemmas

4.15
,
4.13
and
4.12

\displaystyle\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\mectxt[{\mx}]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mectxt[{\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\mx}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mectxt[{\mt}]

∎

As a simple example, consider the following standard equivalence of
let and
\lambda
, which we will need later and prove using the above
lemmas:

Lemma 4.17 (Let-
\lambda
Equivalence).

For any
\menv,\mx:\mA\vdash\mt:\mB
and
\menv\vdash\ms:\mA
,

(\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mt})\,\ms\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtlet{\mx}{\ms}{\mt}

Proof.

First, we lift
\mt
using linearity of evaluation contexts, then an
open
\beta
-reduction, linked by transitivity:

\displaystyle(\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mt})\,\ms

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx}{\ms}{(\mtfun{\mx}{\mA}{\mt})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx}{\ms}{\mt}

∎

The concepts of pure and terminating terms are useful because when
subterms are pure or terminating, they can be moved around to prove
equivalences more easily.

Definition 4.18 (Pure, Terminating Terms).

(1)

A term
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
is
terminating
if for
any closing
\mgamma
, either
\mt[\mgamma]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
or

\mt[\mgamma]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mv
for some
\mv
.

(2)

A term
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
is
pure
if for any
closing
\mgamma
,
\mt[\mgamma]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mv
for some
\mv
.

The following terminology and proof are taken from
(
Führmann 1999
)
.

Lemma 4.19 (Pure Terms are Thunkable).

For any pure
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
,

\mtlet\mx\mt{\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mx}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtfun\my\mB\mt

Proof.

There are two cases
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec},\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}
.

(1)

Let
\mgammaone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{v,\menv}}\mgammatwo
and define

\mtone=\mt{}[\mgammaone]
and
\mttwo=\mt{}[\mgammatwo]
. Then we know
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
.

(a)

If
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}
we’re done.

(b)

It is impossible that
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}}
because
\mt

is terminating.

(c)

If
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}\mvone
, then we know that

\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvtwo
with
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-j}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
. Next,

\mtlet\mx\mtone{\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mx}}\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mvone}

Then it is sufficient to show
\mtfun{\my}{\mB}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-j}_{v,\mB\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mA}}\mtfun{\my}{\mttwo}
, i.e. that for
any
\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{k\leq(i-j)}_{v,\mB}}\mvtwopr
that

(\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mvone})\,\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{k}_{t,\mA}}(\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mttwo})\,\mvtwopr

The left side steps

(\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mvone})\,\mvonepr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mvone

And the right side steps

(\mtfun{\my}{\mB}{\mtone})\,\mvtwopr\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}\mvtwo

And
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{k}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
by assumption above.

(2)

Let
\mgammaone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}^{i-j}_{\menv}\mgammatwo
and define

\mtone=\mt{}[\mgammaone]
and
\mttwo=\mt{}[\mgammatwo]
. Then we know
\mtone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mttwo
.

Since
\mt
is terminating we know
\mtone\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvone
and
for some
k
,
\mttwo\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k}}\mvtwo
.
Then we need to show
And we need to show
{\mtfun\my\mB\mvone}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mB\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mA}}\mtfun\my\mB\mttwo
.
Given any
\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{k\leq i}_{v,\mvtwopr}}
, we need to show

(\mtfun\my\mB\mvone)\,\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{k}_{t,\mB}}(\mtfun\my\mB\mttwo)\,\mvtwopr

The
\beta
reduction takes
0
steps, then
\mttwo
starts
running. If
k>i
, there is nothing left to show. Otherwise,
k\leq i
and we know
\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i-k}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
which is
the needed result.

∎

Lemma 4.20 (Pure Terms are Essentially Values).

If
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
is a pure term, then for any
\menv,\mx:\mA\vdash\ms:\mB
,
\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\ms}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\ms[\mt/\mx]
holds.

Proof.

First, since by open
\beta
we have

(\mtfun{\my}{\munitty}\mt\,\mtunit)\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mt
, by congruence (
lemma

4.8
)

\ms[\mt/\mx]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\ms[(\mtfun{\my}{\munitty}\mt\,\mtunit)\,\mtunit/\mx]

And by reverse
\beta
reduction, this is further equivalent to

\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\mtfun\my\munitty\mt}{\ms[(\mxin{f}\,\mtunit)/\mx]}

By thunkability of
\mt
and a commuting conversion this is equivalent to:

\mtletvert\mx\mt{\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\mtfun\my\munitty\mx}{\ms{}[(\mxin{f}\,\mtunit)/\mx]}}

Which by
\beta
reduction at each
\mx
in
\mx
is:

\mtletvert\mx\mt{\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\mtfun\my\munitty\mx}{\ms[\mx]}}

And a final
\beta
reduction eliminates the auxiliary
\mxin{f}
:

\mtletvert\mx\mt{\ms[\mx]}

∎

Also, since we consider all type errors to be equal, terminating terms
can be reordered:

Lemma 4.21 (Terminating Terms Commute).

If
\menv\vdash\mt:\mA
and
\menv\vdash\mtpr:\mApr
and

\menv,\mx:\mA,\my:\mApr\vdash\ms:\mB
, then

\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\mtlet{\mxpr}{\mtpr}{\ms}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtlet{\mxpr}{\mtpr}{\mtlet{\mx}{\mt}{\ms}}

Proof.

By symmetry it is sufficient to prove one direction.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

Let
\mgammaone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}{i}{\menv}\mgammatwo
.
We need to show

\mtletvert{\mx}{\mt{}[\mgammaone]}{\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]}{\ms{}[\mgammaone]}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mB}}\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]}{\mtletvert{\mx}{\mt{}\mgammatwo}{\ms{}[\mgammatwo]}}

Note that this is true if the left side diverges or errors, so this
is true with no conditions on
\mt,\mtpr

By
corollary

4.10
, we know
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mt[\mgammatwo]
and
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i}_{t,\mApr}}\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]
. We do a joint case analysis on these two
facts.

(a)

If
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}
, done.

(b)

If
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, done.

(c)

If
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}\mvone
, then also

\mt[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvtwo
.

(i)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{(i-j)+1}}
, done.

(ii)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k\leq(i-j)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, done.

(iii)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k\leq(i-j)}}\mvonepr
, then

\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvtwopr
with
\mvonepr\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-(j+k)}_{v,\mApr}}\mvtwopr
and the result follows by

corollary

4.10
for
\ms
because we know

\ms{}[\mgammaone,\mvone/\mx,\mvonepr/\mxpr]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-(j+k)}_{t,\mApr}}\ms{}[\mgammatwo,\mvtwo/\mx,\mvtwopr/\mxpr]

(2)

Let
\mgammaone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{v,\menv}}\mgammatwo
.
We need to show

\mtletvert{\mx}{\mt{}[\mgammaone]}{\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]}{\ms{}[\mgammaone]}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mB}}\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]}{\mtletvert{\mx}{\mt{}\mgammatwo}{\ms{}[\mgammatwo]}}

By
corollary

4.10
, we know
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mA}}\mt[\mgammatwo]
and
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{i}_{t,\mApr}}\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]
. We do a joint case analysis on these two
facts.

(a)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+1}}
, done.

(b)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
and

\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
. In this case we know
the right hand side errors, so we must show the left side
errors. Since
\mt
is
terminating
, either

\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
(done) or
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvone
.
In the latter case we are also done because:

\mtletvert{\mx}{\mt{}[\mgammaone]}{\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]}{\ms{}[\mgammaone]}}\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\mtletvert{\mxpr}{\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]}{\ms{}[\mgammaone,\mv/\mx]}}\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}

(c)

If
\mtpr{}[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq i}}\mvtwopr
then
either
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}}
or

\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{\mvonepr}}\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-j}_{v,\mApr}}\mvtwopr
.
Next, consider
\mt[\mgammatwo]
.

(i)

If
\mt[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{(i-j)+1}}
done.

(ii)

If
\mt[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k\leq(i-j)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, then we
know also that
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, there is
nothing left to show.

(iii)

If
\mt[\mgammatwo]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k\leq(i-j)}}\mvtwo
, then
either
\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
or

\mt[\mgammaone]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvone\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{i-(j+k)}_{v,\mA}}\mvtwo
. If
\mtpr{}[\mgammaone]
or
\mt{}[\mgammaone]

errors, done, otherwise both return values and the
result follows by
corollary

4.10
for
\ms
.

∎

5.
Casts as Embedding-Projection Pairs

In this section, we show how arbitrary casts can be broken down into
embedding-projection
pairs.
First, we define type dynamism and show that casts between less and
more dynamic types form an ep pair.
Then we will show that every cast is a composition of an upcast and a
downcast.

5.1.
Embedding-Projection Pairs

First, we define ep pairs with respect to
logical

approximation.
Note that since logical approximation implies observational error
approximation, these are also ep pairs with respect to observational
error approximation. However, in theorems where we
construct

new ep pairs from old ones, we will need that the input ep pairs are
logical ep pairs, not just observational, because we have not proven
that logical approximation is
complete
for observational error
approximation.
As with casts, we use evaluation contexts for convenience.

Definition 5.1 (EP Pair).

A (logical) ep pair
(\mEin{e},\mEin{p}):\mA\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mB
is a
pair of an
embedding

\mEin{e}[{\cdot:\mA}]:\mB
and a

projection

\mEin{p}[{\cdot:\mB}]:\mA
satisfying

(1)

Retraction:
{\mx:\mA\vdash\mx\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mx}]}]:\mA}

(2)

Projection:
{\my:\mB\vdash\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\my}]}]\sqsubseteq\my:\mB}

Next, we prove that in any embedding-projection pair that embeddings
are pure (always produce a value with no effects) and that projections
are terminating (either error or produce a value).
Paired with the lemmas we have proven about pure and terminating
programs in the previous section, we will be able to prove theorems
about ep pairs more easily.

Lemma 5.2 (Embeddings are Pure).

If
\mEin{e},\mEin{p}:\mA\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mB
is an
embedding-projection pair then
\mx:\mA\vdash\mEin{e}[{\mx}]:\mB
is pure.

Proof.

The ep pair property states that

\mx:\mA\vDash\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mx}]}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mx:\mA

Given any value
\cdot\vdash\mv:\mA
, by Lemma
4.10
, we know

\mv\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\prec}}^{0}_{t,\mA}}\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mv}]}]

and since
\mv\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mv
, this means there exists
\mvpr

such that
\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mv}]}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvpr
,
and since
\mEin{p}
is an evaluation context, this means
there must exist
\mvpr[2]
with

\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mv}]}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mEin{p}[{\mvpr[2]}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvpr
.
∎

Lemma 5.3 (Projections are Terminating).

If
\mEin{e},\mEin{p}:\mA\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mB
is an
embedding-projection pair then
\my:\mB\vdash\mEin{p}[{\my}]:\mA
is terminating.

Proof.

The ep pair property states that

\my:\mB\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\my}]}]\sqsubseteq\my:\mB

Given any
\mv:\mB
, by Lemma
4.10
, we know

\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\mv}]}]\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succ}}^{0}_{t,\mB}}\mv

so therefore either

\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\mv}]}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, which
because
\mEin{e}
is pure means
{\mEin{p}[{\mv}]}\mathrel{\Mapsto}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}
, or
\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\mv}]}]\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvpr
which by strictness of evaluation contexts means

{\mEin{p}[{\mv}]}\mathrel{\Mapsto}\mvpr[2]
for some

\mvpr[2]
.
∎

Crucially, ep pairs can be constructed using simple function
composition.
First, the identity function is an ep pair by reflexivity.

Lemma 5.4 (Identity EP Pair).

For any type
\mA
,
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}:\mA\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mA
.

Second, if we compose the embeddings one way and projections the
opposite way, the result is an ep pair, by congruence.

Lemma 5.5 (Composition of EP Pairs).

For any ep pairs
\mE_{e,1},\mE_{p,1}:\mAone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mAtwo
and

\mE_{e,2},\mE_{p,2}:\mAtwo\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mAin{3}
,

\mE_{e,2}[{\mE_{e,1}}],\mE_{p,1}[{\mE_{p,2}}]:\mAone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mAin{3}
.

5.2.
Type Dynamism

\sA\sqsubseteq\sB

\frac{~}{{\sty}\sqsubseteq{\sty}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Reflexivity}\frac{{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}{\sAtwo}\sqsubseteq{\sAthree}}{{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAthree}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Transitivity}\frac{~}{\sA\sqsubseteq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Dyn-Top}\frac{\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sBone}\sqsubseteq\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={Sum}}\frac{\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{\spairty{\sAone}{\sBone}\sqsubseteq\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={Prod}}\frac{\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{\sfunty{\sAone}{\sBone}\sqsubseteq\sfunty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={Fun}}

Figure 15.
Type Dynamism

Next, we consider type dynamism and its relationship to the casts.
The type dynamism relation is presented in
Figure
15
.
The relation
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
reads as “
\sA
is less dynamic than

\sB
” or “
\sA
is more precise than
\sB
”.
For the purposes of its definition, we can say that it is the least
reflexive and transitive relation such that every type constructor is
monotone and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
is the greatest element.
Even the function type is monotone in both input and output argument
and for this reason, type dynamism is sometimes called
naïve
subtyping

(
Wadler and
Findler 2009
)
.
However, this gives us no
semantic
intuition about what it
could possibly mean.
We propose that
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
should hold when the casts between

\sA
and
\sB
form an embedding-projection pair

\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}},\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}:\sA\mathrel{\triangleleft}\sB
.
We can then view each of the cases of the gradual guarantee as being

compositional rules
for constructing ep pairs.
Reflexivity and transitivity correspond to the identity and
composition of ep pairs, and the monotonicity of types comes from the
fact that
every
functor preserves ep pairs.

Taking this idea further, we can view type dynamism not just as an

analysis
of pre-existing gradual type casts, but by considering
its simple
proof theory
, we can view proofs of type dynamism
as
synthesizing
the definitions of casts.
To accomplish this, we give a
refined
formulation of the proof
theory of type dynamism in Figure
16
, which
includes explicit proof terms
c:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
.
The methodology behind the presentation is to make reflexivity,
transitivity, and the fact that
\mathord{?}
is a greatest element into

admissible
properties of the system, rather than primitive
rules.
First, by making proofs admissible, we see in detail how bigger casts
are built up from small pieces.

Second, this formulation satisfies a
canonicity
property: there
is exactly one proof of any given derivation, which simplifies the
definition of the semantics.
By giving a presentation where derivations are canonical, the typical
“coherence” theorem, that says any two derivations have equivalent
semantics, becomes trivial.
An alternative formulation would define an ep-pair semantics where
reflexivity and transitivity denote identity and composition of ep
pairs, and then prove that any two derivations have equivalent
semantics.
Instead, we define admissible constructions for reflexivity and
transitivity, and then prove a
decomposition lemma

(
lemma

5.11
) that states that the ep-pair semantics
interprets our admissible reflexivity derivation as identity and
transitivity derivation as a composition.
In short, our presentation makes it obvious that the semantics is
coherent, but not that it is built out of composition, whereas the
alternative makes it obvious that the semantics is built out of
composition, but not that it is coherent.

c:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB

\frac{\sty\in\{\sunitty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\}}{id(\sA):{\sty}\sqsubseteq{\sty}}\frac{\sA\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}c:\sty\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{~}{tag({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}):{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}{tag({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})\circ c:\sty\sqsubseteq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}\\
\frac{c:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo d:\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{c\times d:\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}\frac{c:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo d:\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{c+d:\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sBtwo}\frac{c:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo d:\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{c\to d:\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}

Figure 16.
Canonical Proof Terms for Type Dynamism

We present the proof terms for type dynamism in
Figure
16
.
As in presentations of sequent calculus, we include the identity ep
pair (reflexivity) only for the base types
\sunitty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
.
The next rule
tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\circ c:\sA\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
states
that any casts between a non-dynamic type
\sA
and the dynamic type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
are the composition
\circ
of, first, a tagging-untagging
ep-pair with its underlying tag type
tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}):{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and an ep pair from
\sA
to its tag type
c:\sA\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
.
The product, sum, and function rules are written to evoke that their ep
pairs use the functorial action.

As mentioned the proof terms are
canonical
, meaning there is at
most one derivation of any
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
.

Lemma 5.6 (Canonical Type Dynamism Derivations).

Any two derivations
c,d:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
are equal
c=d
.

Proof.

By induction on
c
, noting in each case that exactly one case is
possible.
∎

\begin{array}[]{rcl}id({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})\\
id(\sunitty)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id(\sunitty)\\
id({\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sAtwo})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id(\sAone)\times id(\sAtwo)\\
id({\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwo})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id(\sAone)+id(\sAtwo)\\
id({\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sAtwo})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id(\sAone)\to id(\sAtwo)\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\circ c)\circ d&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\circ(c\circ d)\\
(id(\sA))\circ d&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&d\\
(c\times d)\circ(c^{\prime}\times d^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(c\circ c^{\prime})\times(d\circ d^{\prime})\\
(c+d)\circ(c^{\prime}+d^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(c\circ c^{\prime})+(d\circ d^{\prime})\\
(c\to d)\circ(c^{\prime}\to d^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&(c\circ c^{\prime})\to(d\circ d^{\prime})\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}top({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&id({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})\\
top(\sunitty)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&tag(\sunitty)\circ id(\sunitty)\\
top(\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sB)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})\circ(top(\sA)\times top(\sB))\\
top(\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sB)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})\circ(top(\sA)+top(\sB))\\
top(\sA\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sB)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&tag({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}})\circ(top(\sA)\to top(\sB))\\
\end{array}

Figure 17.
Type Dynamism Admissible Proof Terms

Next, we need to show that the rules in Figure
15
are all

admissible
in the refined system Figure
16
.
The proof of admissibility is given in
Figure
17
.
First, to show reflexivity is
admissible, we construct the proof
id(\sA):\sA\sqsubseteq\sA
.
It is primitive for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and
\sunitty
and we use the congruence
rule to lift the others.
Second, to show transitivity is admissible, for every
d:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
and
c:\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sAin{3}
, we construct their

composite

c\circ d:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAin{3}
by recursion on

c
.
If
c
is a primitive composite with a tag, we use associativity of
composition to push the composite in.
If
c
is the identity, the composite is just
d
.
Otherwise, both
c
and
d
must be between a connective, and we push
the compositions in.
Finally, we show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
is the most dynamic type by
constructing a derivation
top(\sA):\sA\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
for every

\sA
.
For
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
, it is just the identity; for the remaining types, we use the tag ep
pair and compose with lifted uses of
top
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}m\in\{e,p\}\\
\overline{e}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&p\\
\overline{p}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&e\\
\mE_{m,id(\sA)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\\
\mE_{e,tag(\stagty)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}}\\
\mE_{p,tag(\stagty)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mtelsecase{\mtunroll{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\stagty}}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mE_{e,tag(\stagty)\circ d}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mE_{e,tag(\stagty)}[{\mE_{e,d}}]\\
\mE_{p,tag(\stagty)\circ d}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mE_{p,d}[{\mE_{p,tag(\stagty)}}]\\
\mE_{m,c\times c^{\prime}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mE_{m,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mE_{m,c^{\prime}}\\
\mE_{m,c+c^{\prime}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mE_{m,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mE_{m,c^{\prime}}\\
\mE_{m,c\to c^{\prime}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}&\mE_{\overline{m},c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{m,c^{\prime}}\end{array}

Figure 18.
Type Dynamism Cast Translation

Next, we construct a
semantics
for the type dynamism proofs
that justifies the intuition we have given so far; it is presented in
Figure
18
.
Every type dynamism proof
c:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
defines a
pair

of an embedding
\mectxt_{e,c}
and a projection
\mectxt_{p,c}
.
Since many rules are the same for embeddings and projections, we use

m\in\{e,p\}
to abstract over the
mode
of the cases.
We define the
complement
of a mode
\overline{m}
to swap between
embeddings and projections; it is used in the function case.
The primitive identity casts are interpreted as the identity, and the
primitive composition of casts
tag(\stagty)\circ d
is interpreted
as the composition of ep pairs of
tag(\stagty)
and
d
.
The tag type derivation is interpreted by the same definition as the
cast in Figure
10
: tagging puts the correct
sum case and
\mtroll{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{}
, and untagging unwraps if its the
correct sum case and otherwise errors.
We abbreviate this as pattern matching with an “else” clause, where
the else clause stands for all of the clauses that do not match the
tag type
\stagty
.
The desugaring to repeated case statements on sums should be clear.
The product and sum type are just given by their functorial action
with the same mode.
The function type similarly uses its functorial action, but swaps from
embedding to projection or vice-versa on the domain side.
This shows that there is nothing strange about the function rule: it is
the same construction as for subtyping, but constructing arrows back
and forth at the same time.
The fact that contravariant functors are covariant with respect to ep
pairs in this way is
precisely
the reason they are used
extensively in domain theory.

We next verify that these actually are embedding-projection pairs.
To do this, we use the identity and composition lemmas proved before,
but we also need to use
functoriality
of the actions of type
constructors, meaning that the action of the type interacts well with
identity and composition of evaluation contexts.

Lemma 5.7 (Identity Extension).

{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\quad\text{and}\quad{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\quad\text{and}\quad{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}

Proof.

All are instances of
\eta
expansion.
∎

In a call-by-value language, the functoriality rules do not hold in
general for the product functor, but they do for terminating programs
because their order of evaluation is irrelevant.
Also notice that when composing using the functorial action of the
function type
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}
, the composition flips on the domain side,
because the function type is
contravariant
in its domain.

Lemma 5.8 (Functoriality for Terminating Programs).

The following equivalences are true for any well-typed,

terminating
evaluation contexts.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\mectxttwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mectxttwopr)[{\mectxtone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mectxtonepr}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&(\mectxttwo[{\mectxtone}])\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}(\mectxttwopr[{\mectxtonepr}])\\
(\mectxttwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mectxttwopr)[{\mectxtone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mectxtonepr}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&(\mectxttwo[{\mectxtone}])\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}(\mectxttwopr[{\mectxtonepr}])\\
(\mectxttwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mectxttwopr)[{\mectxtone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mectxtonepr}]&\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}&(\mectxtone[{\mectxttwo}])\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}(\mectxttwopr[{\mectxtonepr}])\end{array}

Proof.

(1)

(
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}
) We need to show (after a commuting conversion)

\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{\mtletvert{\my_{f}}{\mtufun{\mx_{a}}{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mx_{a}}])}]}}{{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mectxttwopr[{\my_{f}\,(\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}])}]}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mectxttwopr[{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}]}])}]}]}}

First, we substitute for
\my_{f}
and then lift the argument

\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}]
out and
\beta
reduce:

\displaystyle\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{\mtletvert{\my_{f}}{\mtufun{\mx_{a}}{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mx_{a}}])}]}}{{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mectxttwopr[{\my_{f}\,(\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}])}]}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mectxttwopr[{({\mtufun{\mx_{a}}{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mx_{a}}])}]}})\,(\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}])}]}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mtletvert{\mx_{a}}{\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}]}{\mectxttwopr[{({\mtufun{\mx_{a}}{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mx_{a}}])}]}})\,\mx_{a}}]}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{\mtletvert{\mx_{a}}{\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}]}{\mectxttwopr[{{{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mx_{a}}])}]}}}]}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx_{f}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\mtufun{\my_{a}}{{\mectxttwopr[{{{\mectxtonepr[{\mx_{f}\,(\mectxtone[{\mectxttwo[{\my_{a}}]}])}]}}}]}}}}

(2)

(
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}
) We need to show

\mtcasevert{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{\mectxtone[{\my}]}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}]}}

First, we do a case-of-case commuting conversion, then lift the
discriminees out,
\beta
reduce and restore them.

\displaystyle\mtcasevert{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtcasevert{\mtinj{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}}{\mypr}{\mtcasevert{\mtinjpr{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtletvert{\my}{{{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}}{\mtcasevert{\mtinj\my}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}}}{\mypr}{\mtletvert{\mypr}{{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}{\mtcasevert{\mtinjpr\mypr}{\my}{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}{\mypr}{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{\mtletvert{\my}{{{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}}{{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\my}}]}}}}{\mypr}{\mtletvert{\mypr}{{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}{{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mypr}}]}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtcasevert{\mx}{\my}{{{\mtinj{\mectxttwo[{{\mectxtone[{\my}]}}]}}}}{\mypr}{{{\mtinjpr{\mectxttwo[{{\mectxtonepr[{\mypr}]}}]}}}}}

(3)

(
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}
) We need to show

\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtmatchpairvert{\my}{\mypr}{\mtpair{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}}{\mtpair{\mectxttwo[{\my}]}{\mectxttwopr[{\mypr}]}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtpair{\mectxttwo[{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}]}{\mectxttwopr{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}}}

First, we make the evaluation order explicit, then re-order using
the fact that terminating programs commute
lemma

4.21
.

\displaystyle\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtmatchpairvert{\my}{\mypr}{\mtpair{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}}{\mtpair{\mectxttwo[{\my}]}{\mectxttwopr[{\mypr}]}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}{\mtmatchpairvert{\my}{\mypr}{\mtpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}}{\mtletvert{\mxtwo}{{\mectxttwo[{\my}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxtwopr}{{\mectxttwopr[{\mypr}]}}{\mtpair{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}{\mtletvert{\mxtwo}{{\mectxttwo[{\mxone}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxtwopr}{{\mectxttwopr[{\mxonepr}]}}{\mtpair{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxtwo}{{\mectxttwo[{\mxone}]}}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}{\mtletvert{\mxtwopr}{{\mectxttwopr[{\mxonepr}]}}{\mtpair{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mx}{\mxpr}{\mx_{p}}{\mtpair{\mectxttwo[{\mectxtone[{\mx}]}]}{{{\mectxttwopr[{\mectxtonepr[{\mxpr}]}]}}}}

∎

With these cases covered, we can show the casts given by type dynamism
really are ep pairs.

Lemma 5.9 (Type Dynamism Derivation denotes EP Pair).

For any derivation
c:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
, then
\mE_{e,c},\mE_{p,c}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sB}\rrbracket{}
are an ep pair.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation
c
.

(1)

(Identity)
{\mE_{e,id(\sA)},\mE_{p,id(\sA)}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}}
. This case is immediate by

corollary

4.10
.

(2)

(Composition)

\frac{\mE_{e,c},\mE_{p,c}:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}\mE_{e,c^{\prime}},\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c}}],\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}}]:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}}
. We need to show the retract property:

\mx:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c}[{\mx}]}]}]}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mx:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}

and the projection property:

\my:\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c}[{\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\my}]}]}]}]\sqsubseteq\my:\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}

Both follow by congruence and the inductive hypothesis, we show the projection property:

(inductive hyp, cong
4.8
)

\displaystyle\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c}[{\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\my}]}]}]}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\my}]}]

(inductive hyp)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\my

(3)

(Tag)
\frac{~}{\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}},\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}}{\llbracket{}{\stagty}\rrbracket{}}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sB}\rrbracket{}}
.

The retraction case follows by
\beta
reduction

\displaystyle\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mx}}}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}{\mxin{\stagty}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mx

For the projection case, we need to show

\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\left(\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{\my}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}{\mxin{\stagty}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\right)}}\sqsubseteq\my

First, on the left side, we do a commuting conversion
(
lemma

4.15
) and then use linearity of evaluation
contexts to reduce the cases to error:

\displaystyle\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\left(\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{\my}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}{\mxin{\stagty}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{\my}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}{\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}}{\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtelsecasevert{\mtunroll{\my}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}{\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{\mxin{\stagty}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}

Next, we
\eta
-expand the right hand side

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtunroll{\my}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{case}}\,\mtunroll{\my}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{of}}\\
\quad\mtsum{\sunitty}{\mxin{\sunitty}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{.}}}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\sunitty}{\mxin{\sunitty}}}\\
\quad\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{.}}}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}}}\\
\quad\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{.}}}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}}}\\
\quad\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{.}}}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}\mtsum{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}}{\mxin{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}}}\\
\quad\mtsum{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\}}}}{\mxin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{.}}}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}\mtsum{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\}}}}{\mxin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\}}}}}\\
\end{array}

The result follows by congruence because
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}\sqsubseteq\mt
for any
\mt
.

(4)

(Functions)
\frac{\mE_{e,c},\mE_{p,c}:\sAone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\sAtwo\mE_{e,c^{\prime}},\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}:\sBone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\sBtwo}{\mE_{p,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{e,c^{\prime}},\mE_{e,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\llbracket{}{\sBone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\triangleleft}\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\llbracket{}{\sBtwo}\rrbracket{}}
We prove the projection property, the retraction
proof is similar. We want to show

\my:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\llbracket{}{\sBtwo}\rrbracket{}\vDash(\mE_{e,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{p,c^{\prime}})[{(\mE_{p,c}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{e,c^{\prime}})[{\my}]}]\sqsubseteq\my:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\llbracket{}{\sBtwo}\rrbracket{}

Since embeddings and projections are terminating, we can apply
functoriality Lemma
5.8
to show the left hand side is
equivalent to

((\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{e,c}}])\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}(\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}}]))[{\my}]

which by congruence and inductive hypothesis is
\sqsubseteq
:

({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}})[{\my}]

which by identity extension
lemma

5.7
is equivalent to
\my
.

(5)

(Products) By the same argument as the function case.

(6)

(Sums) By the same argument as the function case.

∎

Next, while we showed that transitivity and reflexivity were
admissible with the
id(\sA)
and
c\circ d
definitions, their
semantics are not given directly by the identity and composition of
evaluation contexts.
We justify this notation by the following theorems.
First,
id(\sA)
is the identity by identity extension.

Lemma 5.10 (Reflexivity Proofs denote Identity).

For every
\sA
,
\mE_{e,id(\sA)}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}
and
\mE_{p,id(\sA)}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\sA
, using the identity extension lemma.
∎

Second, we have our key
decomposition
theorem.
While the composition theorem says that the composition of any two ep
pairs is an ep pair, the
decomposition
theorem is really a
theorem about the
coherence
of our type dynamism proofs.
It says that given any ep pair given by
c:\sAin{1}\sqsubseteq\sAin{3}
, if we can find a middle type
\sAin{2}
, then we can

decompose

c
’s ep pairs into a composition.
This theorem is used extensively, especially in the proof of the
gradual guarantee.

Lemma 5.11 (Decomposition of Upcasts, Downcasts).

For any derivations
c:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
and
c^{\prime}:\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sAthree
,
the upcasts and downcasts given by their composition
c^{\prime}\circ c

are equivalent to the composition of their casts given by
c,c^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\mx:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{e,c^{\prime}\circ c}[{\mx}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{e,c^{\prime}}[{\mE_{e,c}[{\mx}]}]:\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}

\displaystyle\my:\llbracket{}{\sAthree}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{p,c^{\prime}\circ c}[{\my}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,c}[{\mE_{p,c^{\prime}}[{\my}]}]:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}

Proof.

By induction on the pair
c,c^{\prime}
, following the recursive definition
of
c\circ c^{\prime}
.

(1)

(tag(\stagty)\circ c)\circ d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}tag(\stagty)\circ(c\circ d)
. By inductive hypothesis and strict associativity of
composition of evaluation contexts.

(2)

id(\sAone)\circ d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}d
reflexivity.

(3)

(c\times d)\circ(c^{\prime}\times d^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(c\circ c^{\prime})\times(d\circ d^{\prime})

By inductive hypothesis and functoriality
lemma

5.8
.

(4)

(c+d)\circ(c^{\prime}+d^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(c\circ c^{\prime})+(d\circ d^{\prime})

By inductive hypothesis and functoriality
lemma

5.8
.

(5)

(c\to d)\circ(c^{\prime}\to d^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(c\circ c^{\prime})\to(d\circ d^{\prime})

By inductive hypothesis and functoriality
lemma

5.8
.

∎

Finally, now that we have established the meaning of type dynamism
derivations and proven the decomposition theorem, we can
dispense with direct manipulation of derivations.
So we define the following notation for ep pairs that just uses the
types:

Definition 5.12 (EP Pair Semantics).

Given
c:\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
, we define
\mE_{m,\sA,\sB}=\mE_{m,c}
.

5.3.
Casts Factorize into EP Pairs

Next, we show how the upcasts and downcasts are sufficient to
construct all the casts of
\lambda_{G}
.

First, when
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
, the ep pair semantics and the cast
semantics coincide:

Lemma 5.13 (Upcasts and Downcasts are Casts).

If
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
then
\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{e,\sA,\sB}
and
\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sA,\sB}
.

Proof.

By induction following the recursive definition of
\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}

(1)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}
By reflexivity.

(2)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{+}}}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}
By inductive hypothesis and congruence.

(3)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\times}}}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}
By inductive hypothesis and congruence.

(4)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}
By inductive hypothesis and congruence.

(5)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\stagty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mtroll{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\rrbracket{}}{\mtsum{\stagty}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}
By reflexivity

(6)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\stagty}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mtelsecase{\mtunroll{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{\stagty}{\mx}{\mx}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}
By reflexivity.

(7)

(\sA\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\quad\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}]
By inductive hypothesis and decomposition of ep pairs.

(8)

(\sA\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\quad\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sA}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}}[{\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}]
By inductive hypothesis and decomposition of ep pairs.

(9)

(\sA,\sB\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sB}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\quad\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sB}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mtlet{\mx}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}
Not possible that
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
.

∎

Next, we show that the “general” casts of the gradual language can
be
factorized
into a composition of an upcast followed by a
downcast.
First, we show that factorizing through any type is equivalent to
factorizing through the dynamic type, as a consequence of the

retraction
property of ep pairs.

Lemma 5.14 (Any Factorization is equivalent to Dynamic).

For any
\sAone,\sAtwo,\sApr
with
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sApr
and
\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sApr
,

\mE_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,\sApr}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sApr}}]
.

Proof.

By decomposition and the retraction property:

\mE_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{p,\sApr,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sApr,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]}]}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,\sApr}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sApr}}]

∎

By transitivity of equivalence, this means that factorization through one
\sB
is as good as any other.
So to prove that every cast factors as an upcast followed by a
downcast, we can choose whatever middle type is most convenient.
This lets us choose the simplest type possible in the proof.
For instance, when factorizing a function cast

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
, we can use
the function tag type as the middle type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
and
then the equivalence is a simple use of the inductive hypothesis and
the functoriality principle.

Lemma 5.15 (Every Cast Factors as Upcast, Downcast).

For any
\sAone,\sAtwo,\sApr
with
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sApr
and
\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sApr
,
the cast from
\sAone
to
\sAtwo
factors through
\sApr
:

\mx:\llbracket{}{\sA}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sA}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mx}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,\sApr}[{\mE_{e,\sA,\sApr}[{\mx}]}]:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}

Proof.

(1)

If
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
, then we choose
\sApr=\sAtwo
and we need to show that

\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo\sAtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone\sAtwo}}]

this follows by
lemma

5.13
and
lemma

5.10
.

(2)

If
\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sAone
, we use a dual argument to the previous
case. We choose
\sApr=\sAone
and we need to show that

\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAone\sAtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone\sAone}}]

this follows by
lemma

5.13
and
lemma

5.10
.

(3)

\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}

We choose
\sApr={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
.
By inductive hypothesis,

\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]\quad\text{and}\quad\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sBone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]

Then the result holds by functoriality:

\displaystyle\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}

\displaystyle=\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}(\mE_{p,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}])\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}(\mEin{p,\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mEin{e,\sBone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}])

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}(\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{p,\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}})[{\mE_{p,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\to}}}}\mE_{e,\sBone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]

\displaystyle=\mE_{p,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]

(4)

(Products, Sums) Same argument as function case.

(5)

(\sAone,\sAtwo\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}})\quad\mectxt_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mtlet{\mx}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}
We choose
\sApr={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}
, so we need to show:

\mtlet{\mx}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]
.
By embedding, projection decomposition this is equivalent to

\mtlet{\mx}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{[{}{\cdot}]{}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mho}}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}[{\mE_{p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}]}]}]

Which holds by open
\beta
because the embedding

{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}}}
is pure and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\lfloor}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
.

∎

6.
Graduality from EP Pairs

We now define and prove graduality of our cast calculus.
Graduality, briefly stated, means that if a program is changed to make
its types less dynamic, but otherwise the syntax is the same, then the

operational behavior
of the term is ‘‘less
dynamic’’
4
4

4

Here we invoke the meaning of dynamic as
“active”: less dynamic terms are less active in that they kill the
program with a type error where a more dynamic program would have
continued to run.
in that either the new term has the same
behavior as the old, or it raises a type error,
hiding
some
behavior of the original term.
Graduality, like parametricity, says that a certain type of syntactic
change (making types less dynamic) results in a predictable semantic
change (make behavior less dynamic).
We define these two notions as
syntactic
and
semantic

term dynamism.

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}
