\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sttwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}\par\frac{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\senvonepr\sqsubseteq\senvtwopr}{{{{\senvone,\sxone:\sAone,\senvtwo}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwo:\sAtwo,\senvtwopr}}\mathrel{\vdash}\sxone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sxtwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}}\par\frac{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stunit\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stunit:{\sunitty}\sqsubseteq{\sunitty}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\styone}\sqsubseteq{\stytwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwopr:{\styonepr}\sqsubseteq{\stytwopr}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stpair{\stone}{\stonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stpair{\sttwo}{\sttwopr}:{\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}{\stone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{\sttwo}:{\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{{\senvone,{\sxone:\sAone},{\sxonepr:\sApr}}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,{\sxtwo:\sAtwo},{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}}}\mathrel{\vdash}{\stonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{\sttwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stmatchpair{\sxone:\sAone}{\sxonepr:\sApr}{\stone}{\stonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stmatchpair{\sxtwo:\sAtwo}{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}{\sttwo}{\sttwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}{\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{}{\sAtwopr}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stinj{\stermone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stinj{\stermtwo}:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwopr}}{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{}{\sAtwo}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stinjpr{\stermonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stinjpr{\stermtwopr}:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{{\senvone,\sxone:\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwo:\sAtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\ssone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{{\senvone,\sxonepr:\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}}\mathrel{\vdash}\ssonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwopr:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stcase{\stone}{\sxone:\sAone}{\ssone}{\sxonepr:\sAonepr}{\ssonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stcase{\sttwo}{\sxtwo:\sAtwo}{\sstwo}{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}{\sstwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}\par\frac{{{{{\senvone},{\svarone}:\sAone}\sqsubseteq{{\senvtwo},{\svartwo}:\sAtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stfun{\svarone}{\sAone}{\stone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stfun{\svartwo}{\sAtwo}{\sttwo}:{\sfunty{\sAone}{\sBone}}\sqsubseteq{\sfunty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}}}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sfunty{\sAone}{\sBone}}\sqsubseteq{\sfunty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}}{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\ssone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwo:{{\sAone}}\sqsubseteq{{\sAtwo}}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stapp{\stone}{\ssone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stapp{\sttwo}{\sstwo}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}

Figure 19.
Syntactic Term Dynamism

\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo

\frac{~}{\cdot\sqsubseteq\cdot}\frac{\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo}{\senvone,\sxone:\sAone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo,\sxtwo:\sAtwo}

Figure 20.
Environment Dynamism

We present syntactic term dynamism in Figure
19
, based
on the rules of
Siek
et al. 2015
.
Syntactic term dynamism captures the above idea of changing a program
to use less dynamic types.
If
\stone\sqsubseteq\sttwo
, we think of
\sttwo
as being rewritten to

\stone
by changing the types to be less dynamic.
While we will sometimes abbreviate syntactic term dynamism as
\vdash\stone\sqsubseteq\sttwo
, the full form is

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}

and is read as “
\stone
is syntactically less dynamic than

\sttwo
”.
The syntax evokes the invariant that if you rewrite
\sttwo
to use
less dynamic types
\stone
, then its inputs must be given less
dynamic types
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo
and its outputs must be given
less dynamic types
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
.
We extend type dynamism to environment dynamism in
Figure
20
to say
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo
when

\senvone,\senvtwo
have the same length and the corresponding types
are related.
The rules of syntactic term dynamism capture exactly the idea of
“types on the left are less dynamic”.
Viewed order-theoretically, these rules say that all term constructors
are
monotone
in types and terms.

The second piece of graduality is a
semantic formulation
of
term dynamism.
The intuition described above is that
\stone
should be

semantically
less dynamic than
\sttwo
when it has the same
behavior as
\sttwo
except possibly when it errors.
Note that if
\senvone=\senvtwo
and
\sAone=\sAtwo
, this is
exactly what observational error approximation formalizes.
Of course, since we can cast between any two types, we can cast any
term to be of a different type.
Our definition for semantic term dynamism will then be contextual
approximation
up to cast
:

Definition 6.1 (Observational Term Dynamism).

We say
\senvone\vdash\stone:\sBone
is
observationally
less dynamic
than
\senvtwo\vdash\sttwo:\sBtwo
, written

\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo\vDash\stone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\sttwo:\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo
when

\senvone\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\stletvert{\sxin{2,1}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAin{2,1}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAin{1,1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\sxin{1,1}}}{\begin{array}[t]{l}\vdots\\
\stletvert{\sxin{2,n}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAin{2,n}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAin{1,n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\sxin{1,n}}}{\sttwo}\end{array}}:\sBtwo

where
\senvone=\sxin{1,1}:\sAin{1,1},\ldots,\sxin{1,n}:\sAin{1,n}
and
\senvtwo=\sxin{2,1}:\sAin{2,1},\ldots,\sxin{2,n}:\sAin{2,n}
. Or,
abbreviated as:

\senvone\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sBone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone\sqsubseteq^{\text{obs}}\stlet{\senvtwo}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\senvtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\senvone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\senvone}{\sttwo}:\sBtwo

Note that we have chosen to use the two
upcasts
, but there are
three other ways we could have inserted casts to give
\stone,\sttwo

the same type: we can use upcasts or downcasts on the inputs and we
can use upcasts or downcasts on the outputs.
We will show based on the ep-pair property of upcasts and downcasts
that all of these are equivalent (
lemma

6.7
).

We then define graduality to mean that syntactic term dynamism implies
semantic term dynamism:

Theorem 6.2 (Graduality).

If
{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}
, then
{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{obs}}}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}

Proof.

By
lemmas

6.4
,
3.8
,
4.10
and
6.9
.
∎

Next, we present our logical relations method for proving graduality.
First, to prove an approximation result for terms in
\lambda_{G}
, we
will prove approximation for their translations in
\lambda_{T,\mho}
,
justified by our adequacy theorem.
Second, to prove observational approximation, we will use our logical
relation, justified by our soundness theorem.
For that we use the following “logical” formulation of term dynamism.

Definition 6.3 (Logical Term Dynamism).

For any
\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}\vdash\mtone:\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}
and

\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}\vdash\mttwo:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}

with
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo
and
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
, we define

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}
as

{{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mttwo}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}}

where the right hand side is defined analogous to the environment cast

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\senvtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\senvone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}
.

Lemma 6.4 (Logical Term Dynamism implies Observational Term Dynamism).

For any
\senvone\vdash\stone:\sAone
and
\senvtwo\vdash\sttwo:\sAtwo
with
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo
and
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
, if

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}

then

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{obs}}}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}
.

Proof.

By
theorems

4.9
and
4.4
.
∎

Now that we are in the realm of logical approximation, we have all the
lemmas of §
4.3
at our disposal, and we now start
putting them to work.
First, as mentioned before, we show that at least with logical term
dynamism, the use of upcasts was arbitrary; we could have used
downcasts instead.
The property we need is that the upcast and downcast are

adjoint
(in the language of category theory), also known as a

Galois connection
, which is a basic consequence of the
definition of ep pair:

Lemma 6.5 (EP Pairs are Adjoint).

For any ep pair
(\mEin{e},\mEin{p}):\mAone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mAtwo
, and terms

\menv\vdash\mtone:\mAone,,\menv\vdash\mttwo:\mAtwo
,

\menv\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mttwo:\mAtwo\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{iff}\hskip 9.24994pt\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]:\mAone

Proof.

The two proofs are dual .

\frac{\frac{~}{\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]}]:\mAone}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=EP Pair}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{~}{\menv\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mttwo:\mAtwo}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=Assumption}}{\menv\vDash\mEin{p}[{\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]:\mAone}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Congruence}}{\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]:\mAone}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Transitivity}\par\frac{\frac{\frac{~}{\menv\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]:\mAone}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=Assumption}}{\menv\vDash\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]}]:\mAone}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Congruence}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{~}{\menv\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mEin{p}[{\mttwo}]}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mttwo}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=EP Pair}}{\menv\vDash\mEin{e}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mttwo:\mAtwo}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Transitivity}

∎

Lemma 6.6 (Adjointness on Inputs).

If
\menv,\mxone:\mAone\vdash\mtone:\mB
and
\menv,\mxtwo:\mAtwo\vdash\mttwo:\mB
, and
\mEin{e},\mEin{p}:\mAone\mathrel{\triangleleft}\mAtwo
,
then

{{\menv,\mxone:\mAone}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mtlet{\mxtwo}{\mEin{e}[{\mxone}]}{\mttwo}:\mB}\quad\mathrm{iff}\quad{{\menv,\mxtwo:\mAtwo}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtlet{\mxone}{\mEin{p}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mtone}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mttwo:\mB}

Proof.

By a similar argument to
lemma

6.5

∎

Lemma 6.7 (Alternative Formulations of Logical Term Dynamism).

The following are equivalent

(1)

{{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mttwo}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}}

(2)

{{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}}

(3)

{{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{p,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtone}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}}

(4)

{{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{p,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtone}}]\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}{\mttwo}:\llbracket{}{\sAtwo}\rrbracket{}}

Proof.

By induction on
\senvone
, using
lemma

6.5
and Lemma
6.6

∎

Finally, to prove the graduality theorem, we do an induction over all
the cases of syntactic term dynamism.
Most important is the cast case
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sttwo
which is valid when
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo

and
\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo
.
We break up the proof into 4 atomic steps using the factorization of
general casts into an upcast followed by a downcast
(
lemma

5.15
):
\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mectxt_{p,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}}]
.
The four steps are upcast on the left, downcast on the left, upcast on
the right, and downcast on the right.
These are presented as rules for logical dynamism in
Figure
21
.
Each of the inference rules accounts for two cases.
The
Cast-Right
rule says first that if
\mtone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\mttwo:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
that it is OK to cast
\mttwo
to
\sBtwo
, as
long as
\sBtwo
is more dynamic than
\sAone
, and the cast is either
an upcast or downcast.
Here, our explicit inclusion of
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo
in the syntax
of the term dynamism judgment should help: the rule says that adding
an upcast or downcast to
\mttwo
results in a more dynamic term than

\mtone
,
whenever it is even sensible to ask
: i.e., if it were
not the case that
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo
, the judgment would not be
well-formed, so the judgment holds whenever it makes sense!
The
Cast-Left
rule is dual.

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo(\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sBtwo\vee\sBtwo\sqsubseteq\sAtwo)}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mttwo}]:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Cast-Right}\par\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mtone\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo(\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBone\vee\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAone)}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\mE_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\mttwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Cast-Left}\par

Figure 21.
Term Dynamism Upcast, Downcast Rules

These four rules, combined with our factorization of casts into upcast
followed by downcast suffice to prove the congruence rule for casts
(we suppress the context
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo\vDash
, which is the
same in each line):

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{{{{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}\sqsubseteq{{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}}:\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo}}{{{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}\sqsubseteq{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}]}:\sAone\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\hskip 4.5pt\textsc{right=Cast-Right}}{{{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}\sqsubseteq{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}]}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\hskip 4.5pt\textsc{right=Cast-Left}}{{\mE_{p,\sBone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}]\sqsubseteq{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}]}}:\sBone\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=Cast-Left}}{\mE_{p,\sBone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}]\sqsubseteq\mE_{p,\sBtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\mE_{e,\sAtwo,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\mathord{?}}}}[{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}]}]:\sBone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Cast-Right}}{{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone}\rrbracket{}}\sqsubseteq{\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:up-down-factorization}}

Next, we show the 4 rules are valid, as simple consequences of
the ep pair property and the decomposition theorem.
Also note that while there are technically 4 cases, each comes in a
pair where the proofs are exactly dual, so conceptually speaking there
are only 2 arguments.

Lemma 6.8 (Upcast, Downcast Dynamism).

The four rules in Figure
21
are valid.

Proof.

In each case we choose which case of
lemma

6.7
is
simplest.

(1)

Cast-Left
with
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
. We need to show

\mE_{e,\sBone,\sAtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sBone}[{\mtone}]}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mttwo}
. By decomposition and congruence,

\mE_{e,\sBone,\sAtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sBone}[{\mtone}]}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}
so the conclusion holds by
transitivity and the premise.

(2)

Cast-Right
with
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
. We need to show
\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{p,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtone}\sqsubseteq\mE_{p,\sAone,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{p,\sBtwo,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}]
. By
decomposition and congruence,

\mE_{p,\sAone,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{p,\sBtwo,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]
, so the conclusion
holds by transitivity and the premise.

(3)

Cast-Left
with
\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
. We need to show
\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAone}[{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{p,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtone}}]\sqsubseteq\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]
. By decomposition,

\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAone}[{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}]
, so by
transitivity it is sufficient to show

\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAone}[{\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{p,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtone}}]\sqsubseteq\mE_{p,\sBone,\sAone}[{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mttwo}]}]

which follows by congruence and the premise.

(4)

Cast-Right
with
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo\sqsubseteq\sBtwo
.
We need to show
\mE_{e,\sAone,\sBtwo}[{\mtone}]\sqsubseteq\mE_{e,\sAtwo,\sBtwo}[{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mttwo}}]
.

By decomposition,
\mE_{e,\sAone,\sBtwo}[{\mtone}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mE_{e,\sAtwo,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mtone}]}]
, so
by transitivity it is sufficient to show

\mE_{e,\sAtwo,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mtone}]}]\sqsubseteq\mE_{e,\sAtwo,\sBtwo}[{\mtlet{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mttwo}}]

which follows by congruence and the premise.

∎

Finally, we prove the graduality theorem by induction on syntactic
term dynamism derivations, finishing the proof of

theorem

6.2
.

Theorem 6.9 (Logical Graduality).

If
\senvone\sqsubseteq\senvtwo\vdash\stone\sqsubseteq\sttwo:\sAone
, then

{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}
.

Proof.

By induction on syntactic term dynamism rules.

(1)

To show To show

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\stone}\rrbracket{}\mathrel{\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}}\llbracket{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}\sttwo}\rrbracket{}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}

we use
lemma

6.8
and the argument above.

(2)

\frac{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBone\senvonepr\sqsubseteq\senvtwopr}{{{{\senvone,\sxone:\sAone,\senvtwo}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwo:\sAtwo,\senvtwopr}}\mathrel{\vDash}\sxone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sxtwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}}

We need to show:

\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxone}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxtwo}

Since embeddings are pure
lemmas

5.2
and
4.20
we
can substitute them in and then the two sides are literally the
same.

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxtwo}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mxtwo[\mE_{e,\sAone^{i},\sAtwo^{i}}[{\mxone^{i}}]/\mxtwo^{i}]=\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxone}]

(3)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}{\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{}{\sAtwopr}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stinj{\stermone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stinj{\stermtwo}:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}}

Expanding definitions, we need to show:

\mtcasevert{\mtinj{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}{\mx}{\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mx}]})}{\mx}{\mtinjpr({\mE_{e,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxpr}]})}\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}

By open
\beta
(
lemma

4.13
), the left side can be
reduced, which we can then substitute into due to linearity of
evaluation contexts (
lemma

4.16
):

\displaystyle\mtcase{\mtinj{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}{\mx}{\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mx}]})}{\mx}{\mtinjpr({\mE_{e,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxpr}]})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mx}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mx}]})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}(\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mx}]}))[\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}/\mx]

\displaystyle=\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]})

So by transitivity it is sufficient to show

\mtinj({\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]})\sqsubseteq\mtinj{\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)}

which follows by congruence (
lemma

4.8
).

(4)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwopr}}{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{}{\sAtwo}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stinjpr{\stermonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stinjpr{\stermtwopr}:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}}

essentially the same as the previous case.

(5)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\ssumty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\ssumty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}\hskip 8.19447pt{{{\senvone,\sxone:\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwo:\sAtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\ssone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}\hskip 8.19447pt{{{\senvone,\sxonepr:\sAonepr}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}}\mathrel{\vDash}\ssonepr\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwopr:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stcase{\stone}{\sxone:\sAone}{\ssone}{\sxonepr:\sAonepr}{\ssonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stcase{\sttwo}{\sxtwo:\sAtwo}{\sstwo}{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}{\sstwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}

Expanding definitions, we need to show

\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinj\mxone}{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinjpr\mxonepr}{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinj\mxtwo}{\llbracket{}{\mstwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinjpr\mxtwopr}{\llbracket{}{\mstwopr}\rrbracket{}}}

First, we do some simple rewrites: on the left side, we use a
commuting conversion to push the embedding into the continuations:

\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinj\mxone}{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinjpr\mxonepr}{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mxone}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxonepr}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}

And on the right side we use the fact that embeddings are pure and
so can be moved freely:

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinj\mxtwo}{\llbracket{}{\mstwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mtinjpr\mxtwopr}{\llbracket{}{\mstwopr}\rrbracket{}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcasevert{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mxtwo}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mstwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mxtwopr}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mstwopr}\rrbracket{}}}

Next as with many of the elim forms, we “ep-expand” the
discriminee on the left side, and then simplify based on the
definition of

\mE_{p,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\cdot}]
, using
the case-of-case commuting conversion and open
\beta

lemmas

4.15
and
4.13
:

\displaystyle\mtcasevert{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mxone}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxonepr}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcasevert{\mE_{p,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\stone}]}]}{\mxone}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxonepr}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}

(definition)

\displaystyle=\mtcasevert{\left({\mtcasevert{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\stone}]}{\mxtwo}{\mtinj{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}}{\mxtwopr}{\mtinj{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}}}\right)}{\mxone}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mxonepr}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}

(comm conv
4.15
, open
\beta

4.13
)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtcasevert{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\stone}]}{\mxtwo}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}}}{\mxtwopr}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

Then the final step follows by congruence and adjointness on
inputs
lemmas

4.8
and
6.6
:

\mtcasevert{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAonepr,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{+}}}\sAtwopr}[{\stone}]}{\mxtwo}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msone}\rrbracket{}}]}}{\mxtwopr}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\msonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}\sqsubseteq\mtcasevert{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mxtwo}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mstwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mxtwopr}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mstwopr}\rrbracket{}}}

(6)

\frac{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stunit\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stunit:{\sunitty}\sqsubseteq{\sunitty}}}
.
Expanding we need to show

\mE_{e,\sunitty,\sunitty}[{\mtunit}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtunit}

By definition, the left side is just
\mtunit
and the right side
after a substitution, valid because embeddings are pure

lemmas

5.2
and
4.20
.

(7)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\hskip 8.19447pt{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\ssone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stpair{\stone}{\ssone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stpair{\sttwo}{\sstwo}:{{\sAone}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\sBone}}\sqsubseteq{{\sAtwo}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}{\sBtwo}}}}
. Expanding definitions, we need to show

\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}[{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}}

On the right, we duplicate the embeddings, justified by

lemmas

5.2
and
4.20
, to set up congruence:

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtpair{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}}

On the left, we use linearity of evaluation contexts to lift the
terms out, then perform some open
\beta
reductions and put the
terms back in:

\displaystyle\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\times}}}\sBtwo}[{\mtpair{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\sx}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\sy}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtmatchpair{\sx}{\sy}{\mtpair{\sx}{\sy}}{\mtpair{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\sx}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\sy}]}}}}

\displaystyle(\text{open $\beta$, \ref{lem:open-beta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\sx}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\sy}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{{\mtpair{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\sx}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\sy}]}}}}

\displaystyle(\text{linearity, \ref{lem:evctx-linear}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtpair{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]}}

With the final step following by congruence (
lemma

4.8
) and the premise:

{\mtpair{\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]}}\sqsubseteq\mtpair{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}}

(8)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}{\stone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{\sttwo}:{\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr}}\sqsubseteq{\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}}\hskip 8.19447pt{{{\senvone,{\sxone:\sAone},{\sxonepr:\sApr}}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo,{\sxtwo:\sAtwo},{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}}}\mathrel{\vDash}{\stonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{\sttwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stmatchpair{\sxone:\sAone}{\sxonepr:\sApr}{\stone}{\stonepr}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stmatchpair{\sxtwo:\sAtwo}{\sxtwopr:\sAtwopr}{\sttwo}{\sttwopr}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}
Expanding definitions, we need to show

\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{{\mtmatchpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}}}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtmatchpair{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sttwopr}\rrbracket{}}}:\llbracket{}{\sBtwo}\rrbracket{}

On the right side, in anticipation of a use of congruence, we push
the embeddings in
lemmas

5.2
and
4.20
:

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtmatchpair{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\sttwopr}\rrbracket{}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwopr}\rrbracket{}}}

On the left side, we perform a commuting conversion, ep
expand the discriminee and do some open
\beta
reductions to
simplify the expression.

\displaystyle\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{{\mtmatchpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtmatchpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

\displaystyle(\text{ep pair, \ref{lem:dyn-der-ep}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

(definition)

\displaystyle={\mtmatchpairvert{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtpair{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwo}]}}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

\displaystyle(\text{linearity, \ref{lem:evctx-linear}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mtpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}}}}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}

\displaystyle(\text{comm. conv., \ref{lem:comm-conv}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxone}{\mxonepr}{\mtpair{\mxone}{\mxonepr}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}}}}

\displaystyle(\text{open $\beta$, \ref{lem:open-beta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}}}

The final step is by congruence and adjointness on inputs (
lemmas

4.8
and
6.6
):

{\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\mE_{e,\spairty{\sAone}{\sAonepr},\spairty{\sAtwo}{\sAtwopr}}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mtletvert{\mxonepr}{\mE_{p,\sAonepr,\sAtwopr}[{\mxtwopr}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stonepr}\rrbracket{}}]}}}}\sqsubseteq\mtmatchpairvert{\mxtwo}{\mxtwopr}{\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwopr}\rrbracket{}}}

(9)

\frac{{{{{\senvone},{\svarone}:\sAone}\sqsubseteq{{\senvtwo},{\svartwo}:\sAtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stfun{\svarone}{\sAone}{\stone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stfun{\svartwo}{\sAtwo}{\sttwo}:{\sfunty{\sAone}{\sBone}}\sqsubseteq{\sfunty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}}}
.
Expanding definitions, we need to show

\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\mtfun{\mvarone}{\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}\,(\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}])}]}}\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\llbracket{}{\mttwo}\rrbracket{}}}

First we simplify by performing some open
\beta
reductions on
the left and let-
\lambda
equivalence and a commuting conversion
(
lemmas

4.13
,
4.17
and
4.15
):

\displaystyle\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\mtfun{\mvarone}{\llbracket{}{\sAone}\rrbracket{}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}\,(\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}])}]}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\llbracket{}{\mtone}\rrbracket{}}}]}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\mtone}\rrbracket{}}]}}}

and on the right, we move the embedding into the body, which is
justified because embeddings are essentially values
(
lemmas

5.2
and
4.20
):

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\llbracket{}{\mttwo}\rrbracket{}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mttwo}\rrbracket{}}}

The final step is justified by congruence
lemma

4.8
and adjointness on inputs
lemma

6.6
and the premise:

{\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mtletvert{\mxone}{\mE_{p,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxtwo}]}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\mtone}\rrbracket{}}]}}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtfun{\mxtwo}{\mAtwo}{\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\mttwo}\rrbracket{}}}

(10)

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sfunty{\sAone}{\sBone}}\sqsubseteq{\sfunty{\sAtwo}{\sBtwo}}}{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\ssone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sstwo:{{\sAone}}\sqsubseteq{{\sAtwo}}}}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vDash}\stapp{\stone}{\ssone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\stapp{\sttwo}{\sstwo}:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}
.
Expanding definitions, we need to show

\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}\vDash\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\,\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}\,\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}:\llbracket{}{\sBtwo}\rrbracket{}

First, we duplicate the embedding on the right hand side,
justified by purity of embeddings, to set up a use of congruence
later:

\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}\,\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)

Next, we use linearity of evaluation contexts

lemma

4.16
so that we can do reductions at the
application site without worrying about evaluation order:

\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}\,\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\mxin{a}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}\,\mxin{a}}]}}

Next, we ep-expand
\mxin{f}
(
lemma

5.9
) and perform
some
\beta
reductions, use the ep property and then reverse the
use of linearity.

\displaystyle\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\mxin{a}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}\,\mxin{a}}]}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\mxin{a}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{p,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}}]}]\,\mxin{a}}]}}

\displaystyle(\text{open $\beta$, \ref{lem:open-beta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\mxin{a}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\mE_{p,\sBone,\sBtwo}[{\left({\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}}]}\right)\,\left(\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxin{a}}]\right)}]}]}}

\displaystyle(\text{ep pair, \ref{lem:dyn-der-ep}})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mtletvert{\mxin{f}}{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}{\mtletvert{\mxin{a}}{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}{\left({\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\mxin{f}}]}\right)\,\left(\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\mxin{a}}]\right)}}

\displaystyle(\text{linearity, \ref{lem:evctx-linear}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\left({\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}\right)\,\left(\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]\right)

With the final step being congruence
lemma

4.8
:

\left({\mE_{e,\sAone\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBone,\sAtwo\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\to}}}\sBtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\stone}\rrbracket{}}]}\right)\,\left(\mE_{e,\sAone,\sAtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\ssone}\rrbracket{}}]\right)\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sttwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)\left(\mtletvert{\llbracket{}{\senvtwo}\rrbracket{}}{\mE_{e,\senvone,\senvtwo}[{\llbracket{}{\senvone}\rrbracket{}}]}{\llbracket{}{\sstwo}\rrbracket{}}\right)

∎

7.
Related Work and Discussion

Our analysis of graduality as observational approximation and dynamism
as ep pairs builds on the axiomatic and denotational semantics of
graduality for a call-by-name language presented in

(
New and Licata 2018
)
.
The semantics there gives axioms of type and term dynamism that imply that
upcasts and downcasts are embedding-projection pairs.
Our analysis here is complementary: we present the graduality theorem
as a concrete property of a gradual language defined with an
operational semantics.
Our graduality logical relation should serve as a concrete

model
of a call-by-value version of gradual type theory,
similar to the call-by-name denotational models presented there.
Furthermore, we show here how this interpretation of graduality maps
back to a standard cast calculus presentation of gradual typing.

Graduality vs Gradual Guarantee

The notion of graduality we present here is based on the
dynamic
gradual guarantee
by Siek, Vitousek, Cimini, and Boyland

(
Siek
et al. 2015
;
Boyland 2014
)
. The dynamic gradual guarantee says that
syntactic term dynamism is an
invariant
of the operational
semantics up to error on the less dynamic side.
More precisely, if
\cdot\vdash t_{1}\sqsubseteq t_{2}:A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2}
then
either
t_{1}\mapsto^{*}\mho
or both
t_{1},t_{2}
diverge or
t_{1}\mapsto^{*}v_{1}
and
t_{2}\mapsto^{*}v_{2}
with
v_{1}\sqsubseteq v_{2}
.
Observe that when restricting
A_{1}=A_{2}=1
, this is precisely
the relation on closed programs out of which we build our definition
of semantic term dynamism.
We view their formulation of the dynamic gradual guarantee as a
syntactic
proof technique
for proving graduality of the system.

Graduality should be easier to formulate for different presentations
of gradual typing because it does not require a second syntactic
notion of term dynamism for the implementation language.
In the proofs of the gradual guarantee in
Siek
et al. 2015
, they have to
develop new rules for term dynamism for their cast calculus, that they
do not attempt to justify at an intuitive level.
Additionally, they have to change their translation from the gradual
surface language to the cast calculus, because the traditional
translation did not preserve the rigid syntactic formulation of term
dynamism.
In more detail, when a dynamically typed term
t:\mathord{?}
was applied to a
term
s:A
, in their original formulation this was translated as

\llbracket{}{t~s}\rrbracket{}=(\langle(A\to\mathord{?})\Leftarrow\mathord{?}\rangle\llbracket{}{t}\rrbracket{})\>\llbracket{}{s}\rrbracket{}

but if the term in function position had a function type
t^{\prime}:\mathord{?}\to\mathord{?}
, it was translated as

\llbracket{}{t^{\prime}\,s}\rrbracket{}=(\llbracket{}{t}\rrbracket{}\>(\langle\mathord{?}\Leftarrow A\rangle\llbracket{}{s}\rrbracket{})

But if
t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq t
, we would not have
\llbracket{}{t^{\prime}s}\rrbracket{}\sqsubseteq\llbracket{}{ts}\rrbracket{}
because the function position on the left has type
\mathord{?}\to\mathord{?}
which is
more dynamic
than on the right which has
A\to\mathord{?}
.
While changing this was perfectly reasonable to do to use their
syntactic proof method, we can see that from the
semantic
point
of view of graduality there was nothing wrong with their original
translation and it could have been validated using a logical relation.

Another significant difference between our work and theirs is that we
identify the central role of embedding-projection pairs in graduality,
and take advantage of it in our proof.
As mentioned above, they add rules to term dynamism for the cast
calculus without justification.
These rules are the generalization of our
Cast-Right
and

Cast-Left

without
the restriction that the casts be
upcasts or downcasts:

\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\sAone\sqsubseteq\sBtwo}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{\sttwo}:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sBtwo}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Cast-Right'}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}\stone\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sAone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}\sBone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo}{{{{\senvone}\sqsubseteq{\senvtwo}}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sBone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}}{\stone}\mathrel{\sqsubseteq}\sttwo:{\sBone}\sqsubseteq{\sAtwo}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=Cast-Left'}

These are valid rules in our system, but by identifying the subset of
upcasts and downcasts, we prove the validity of the rules from
earlier, intuitive rules: decomposition, congruence, and the ep-pair
properties.
Furthermore, while we do not take these rules as primitive it is
notable that these two rules imply that upcasts and downcasts are
adjoint—i.e., if
\sAone\sqsubseteq\sAtwo
, the following are provable for

\st:\sAone
and
\ss:\sAtwo
:

\st\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\st}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAtwo}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAone}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\langle{\sAone}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\Leftarrow}}}{\sAtwo}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\ss}}\sqsubseteq\ss

Siek
et al. 2015
also present a theorem called the

static
gradual guarantee that pertains to the type checking of
gradually typed programs.
The static gradual guarantee says that if a term
\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:A_{1}
type checks, and
t_{2}
is syntactically more dynamic, then
\Gamma\vdash t_{2}:A_{2}
with a more dynamic type, i.e.,
A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2}
.
We view this as a
corollary
to graduality. If type checking is
a compositional procedure that seeks to rule out dynamic type errors,
then if
t_{1}
is syntactically less dynamic than
t_{2}
, then it is
also semantically less dynamic, meaning every type error in
t_{2}
’s
behavior was already present in
t_{1}
, so it should also type check.

Types as EP Pairs

The interpretation of types as retracts of a single domain originated
in
Scott 1972
and is a common tool in denotational semantics,
especially in the presence of a convenient
universal
domain.
A retraction is a pair of morphisms
s:A\to B
,
r:B\to A

that satisfy the retraction property
r\circ s=\text{id}_{A}
, but not
necessarily the projection property
s\circ r\mathrel{\sqsubseteq_{\text{err}}}\text{id}_{B}
. Thus ep pair semantics can be seen as a more refined
retraction semantics.
Retractions have been used to study interaction between typed and
untyped languages, e.g., see
Benton 2005
;
(Favonia)
et al. 2017
.

Embedding-projection pairs are used extensively in domain theory as a
technical device for solving non-well-founded domain equations, such
as the semantics of a dynamic type.
In this paper, our error-approximation ep pairs do not play this role,
and instead the retraction and projection properties are desirable in
their own right for their intuitive meaning for type checking.

Many of the properties of our embedding-projection pairs are
anticipated in
Henglein 1994
and
Thatte 1990
.

Henglein 1994
defines a language with a notion of
coercion

A\rightsquigarrow B
that corresponds to general casts, with primitives
of tagging
tc!:tc(\mathord{?},\ldots)\rightsquigarrow\mathord{?}
and untagging

tc?:\mathord{?}\rightsquigarrow tc(\mathord{?},\ldots)
for every type
constructor “
tc
”.
Crucially, Henglein notes that
tc!;tc?
is the identity modulo efficiency
and that
tc?;tc!
errors more than the identity.
Furthermore, they define classes of “positive” and “negative”
coercions that correspond to embeddings and projections, respectively,
and a “subtyping” relation that is the same as type precision.
They then prove several theorems analogous to our results:

(1)

(Retraction) For any pair of positive coercion
p:A\rightsquigarrow B
,
and negative coercion
n:B\rightsquigarrow A
, they show that

p;n
is equal to the identity in their equational theory.

(2)

(Almost projection) Dually, they show that
n;p
is equal to
the identity
assuming
that
tc?;tc!
is equal to the identity
for every type constructor.

(3)

They show every coercion factors as a positive cast to
\mathord{?}

followed by a negative cast to
\mathord{?}
.

(4)

They show that
A\leq B
if and only if there exists a positive
coercion
A\rightsquigarrow B
and a negative coercion
B\rightsquigarrow A
.

They also prove factorization results that are similar to our
factorization definition of semantic type precision, but it is unclear
if their theorem is stronger or weaker than ours.
One major difference is that their work is based on an equational
theory of casts, whereas ours is based on notions of observational
equivalence and approximation of a standard call-by-value language.
Furthermore, in defining our notion of observational error approximation,
we provide a more refined projection property, justifying their use of
the term “safer” to compare
p;e
and the identity.

The system presented in
Thatte 1990
, called “quasi-static typing”
is a precursor to gradual typing that inserts type annotations into
dynamically typed programs to make type checking explicit.
There they prove a desirable soundness theorem that says their type
insertion algorithm produces an explicitly coercing term that is
minimal in that it errors no more than the original dynamic term.
They prove this minimality theorem with respect to a partial order

\sqsupseteq
defined as a logical relation over a domain-theoretic
semantics that (for the types they defined) is analogous to our error
ordering for the operational semantics.
However, they do not define our operational formulation of the
ordering as contextual approximation, linked to the denotational
definition by the adequacy result, nor that any casts form embedding-projection
pairs with respect to this ordering.

Finally, we note that neither of these papers

(
Henglein 1994
;
Thatte 1990
)
extends the analysis to
anything like graduality.

Semantics of Casts

Superficially similar to the embedding-projection pair semantics are
the
threesome casts
of
Siek and Wadler 2010
.
A threesome cast factorizes an arbitrary cast
A\Rightarrow B

through a third type
C
as a
downcast

A\Rightarrow C

followed by an
upcast

C\Rightarrow B
, whereas ep-pair semantics
factorizes a cast as an
upcast

A\Rightarrow\mathord{?}
followed by a

downcast

\mathord{?}\Rightarrow B
.
Threesome casts can be used to implement gradual typing in a
space-efficient manner, the third type
C
is used to collapse a
sequence of arbitrarily many casts into just the two.
In the general case, the threesome cast
A\Rightarrow C\Rightarrow B
is
stronger
(fails more) than the direct cast
A\Rightarrow B
.
This is the point of threesome casts: the middle type faithfully
represents a sequence of casts in minimal space.
EP pair semantics instead factorizes a cast
A\Rightarrow B
into an

upcast

A\Rightarrow\mathord{?}
followed by a
downcast

\mathord{?}\Rightarrow B
, a factorization already utilized in

(
Henglein 1994
)
, and which we showed is

always
equivalent to the direct cast
A\Rightarrow B
.
We view the benefits of the techniques as orthogonal: the up-down
factorization helps to prove graduality, whereas the down-up
factorization helps implementation.
The fact that both techniques reduce reasoning about arbitrary casts
to just upcasts and downcasts supports the idea that upcasts and
downcasts are a fundamental aspect of gradual typing.

Recently, work on
dependent interoperability

(
Dagand
et al. 2016
;
Dagand
et al. 2018
)
has identified Galois connections as a semantic
formulation for casting between more and less precise types in a
non-gradual dependently typed language, and conjectures that this
should relate to type dynamism.
We confirm their conjecture in showing that the casts in gradual
typing satisfy the slightly stronger property of being
embedding-projection pairs and have used it to explain the cast semantics
of gradual typing and graduality.
Furthermore, our analysis of the precision rules as compositional
constructions on ep pairs is directly analogous to their library,
which implements “connections” between, for instance, function types
given connections between the domains and codomains using Coq’s
typeclass mechanism.

Pairs of Projections and Blame

One of the main inspirations for this work is the analysis of
contracts in
Findler and Blume 2006
.
They decompose contracts in untyped languages as a pair of
“projections”, i.e., functions
c:\mathord{?}\to\mathord{?}
satisfying
c\mathrel{\sqsubseteq_{\mathord{?}\to\mathord{?}}}\text{id}
.
However, they do not provide a rigorous definition or means to prove
this ordering for complex programs as we have.
There is a close relationship between such projections and ep pairs
(an instance of the relationship between adjunctions and (co)monads):
for any ep pair
e,p:A\mathrel{\triangleleft}B
,
e\circ p:B\to B
is a
projection.
However, we think this relationship is a red herring: instead we think
that a pair of projections is better understood as ep pairs
themselves.
The intuition they present is that one of the projections restricts
the behavior of the “positive” party (the term) and the other
restricts the behavior of the “negative” party (the continuation).
EP pairs are similar, the projection restricts the positive party by
directly checking, and the embedding restricts the negative party in
the function case by calling a projection on any value received from
its continuation.
However, in our current formulation, it does not even make sense to ask
if each component of our embedding-projection pairs is a projection
because the definition of a projection assumes that the domain and
codomain are the same (to define the composite
c\circ c
).
We conjecture that this can be made sensible by using a PER semantics
where types are relations on untyped values, so that the embedding and
projection have “underlying” untyped terms representing them, and
those are projections.

Their analysis of blame was adapted to gradual typing in

Wadler and
Findler 2009
and plays a complementary role to our
analysis: they use the dynamism relation to help prove the
blame soundness theorem, whereas we use it to prove graduality.
The fact that they use essentially the same solution suggests there is

a deeper connection between blame and graduality than is currently
understood.

Gradualization

The Gradualizer
(
Cimini and Siek 2016
;
Cimini and Siek 2017
)
and Abstracting
Gradual Typing (AGT)
(
Garcia
et al. 2016b
)
both seek to make language design
for gradually typed languages more systematic.
In doing so they make proving graduality far easier than our proof
technique possibly could: it holds by construction.
Furthermore, these systems also provide a surface-level syntax for
gradual typing and an explanation for gradual type checking, while we
do not address these at all.
However, the downside of their approaches is that they require a rigid
adherence to a predefined language framework.
While our gradual cast calculus as presented fits into this framework,
many gradually typed languages do not.
For instance, Typed Racket, the first gradually typed language ever
implemented
(
Tobin-Hochstadt
and Felleisen 2008
)
, is not given an operational
semantics in the style of a cast calculus, but rather is given a
semantics
by translation
to an untyped language using
contracts.
We could prove the graduality of such a system by adapting our logical
relation to an untyped setting.

We hope in the future to explore the connections between the above
frameworks and our analysis of dynamism as embedding-projection pairs.
We conjecture that both Gradualizer and AGT by construction produce
upcasts and downcasts that satisfy the ep pair properties.
The AGT approach in particular has some similarities that stand out:
their formulation of type dynamism is based on an embedding-projection
pair between static types and sets of gradual types.
However, we are not sure if this is a coincidence or has a deeper
connection to our approach.

8.
Conclusion

Graduality is a key property for gradually typed languages as it
validates programmer intuition that adding precise types only results
in stricter type checking.
Graduality is challenging to prove.
Moreover, it rests upon the language’s
definition of type dynamism but there has been little guidance on
defining type dynamism, other than that graduality must hold.
We have given a semantics for type dynamism:
\sA\sqsubseteq\sB
should
hold when the casts between
\sA,\sB
form an embedding-projection
pair.
This allows for natural proofs of graduality using a logical relation
for observational error approximation.

Looking to the future, we would like to make use of our semantic
formulation of type dynamism based on ep pairs to design and analyze
gradual languages with advanced features such as parametric
polymorphism, effect tracking, and mutable state.
For parametric polymorphism in particular, we would like to
investigate whether our approach justifies any of the type-dynamism
definitions previously proposed
(
Ahmed
et al. 2017
;
Igarashi
et al. 2017a
)
, and the
possibility of proving both graduality and parametricity theorems with
a single logical relation.
